- **Linux** (`linuxSecretServiceStore`, `protectedFileStore`): the Secret Service through `secret-tool` where a session bus exists; otherwise the permission-checked file fallback: a 0700 directory and 0600 files owned by the NimbleNewt user, names hex-encoded, written atomically, and every read opens the file without following a link and judges the open file itself (owner, mode, kind), refusing anything broader or any link by name; a link at the directory or the file is never trusted. The Secret Service store refuses a value that ends in a line break (the tool's output cannot tell it from its own). `chooseSecretStore` picks per host (Keychain, DPAPI, Secret Service when it answers, else the files), writes the choice to `<dataDir>/secret-store.json` and says why. Later starts use the recorded store; if it cannot be used the start fails naming it, so secrets never quietly look missing because the store changed.
- **Errors** are `SecretStoreError` with a code (`unavailable`, `locked`, `unsafe`, `invalid`) and the reference name; none of them contains a value, and `set` returns nothing.
- **Tested here and not here.** The file store runs on this Linux server and in CI; the macOS and Windows backends run in the hosted CI on macOS and Windows; the Secret Service needs a desktop session and is skipped on the server and CI. **Not verified** (they need real hardware and an unattended service identity, so they belong to the real-hardware QA, Y02): that references resolve under the service account after logout and reboot on macOS, Windows and Linux, and a locked login keychain on a headless Mac.
- **Not here.** The "written through the API, chat or UI" path (a state API operation and the settings view: U08) and wiring the chosen store into `startController`; export and backup already carry references only (F02).

## A03 notes, part 3: quarantine, and the split of the Windows launcher (2026-10-22)

A03 is now the launcher core, the Linux (bubblewrap) and macOS (Seatbelt) backends, the recipe with its revisions, trusted-host mode, capacity-after-health, and this part: the quarantine. The Windows Sandbox launcher and everything specific to it moved to the new card **A03b** (Windows Sandbox launcher; blocked by A03, and Y02 now waits for it), because it needs real Windows hardware to qualify and should not hold back the cards that need only the Linux and macOS launchers. The escape fixture "on each OS" therefore means each OS that has a backend; Windows has its own in A03b. This is a plan change for the owner to confirm.

`packages/worker/src/sandbox/quarantine.ts`, 6 tests (POSIX-only checks skip on Windows and as root):

- `quarantine({ root, path, reason, by })` moves a directory the agent lost into `root/<id>/content` with a `manifest.json` (where it came from, why, by whom, when, counts of files, directories, links and bytes), then reduces every permission to read-only and keeps the root owner-only. Nothing deletes or merges it, and nothing here puts it back: release is a deliberate owner action elsewhere. The same place quarantined twice keeps both.
- A move on one filesystem is a rename; across filesystems the tree is copied, checked against what was counted and only then removed from the old place; any failure leaves the original where it was. Links stay links and are never followed (their targets keep their permissions and content).
- `readQuarantined` is the owner's read: a path that leaves the quarantined directory, goes through a link, or is not a quarantine ID is refused. `listQuarantined` lists the manifests, oldest first.
- The caller must have stopped everything that could write to the directory (the freeze and settle order is H12's); who may call quarantine and when a lost path triggers it is H12/U08.
- **F09 limits (Opus rounds on the macOS chunking).** If the keychain cannot be read back after a write, nothing is cleaned up and the error is raised (the state is unknown). If putting the previous header back fails after a half-written value, the entry stays damaged and is refused by name at the next read, never returned. The macOS logic is tested against an in-memory stand-in for `security` on every platform and against the real tool on the hosted macOS job.

## T01 notes: the immutable tool and skill catalog (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/catalog/` (`CatalogService`), 24 tests on four backends (mutation-checked: a skill able to run, no grant needed, personal artifacts visible to all, usage unguarded, usage not filtered by project each fail tests). The controller now depends on `@nimblenewt/registry` for the canonical naming of F07 (reserved `nimblenewt` and `toolbox` namespaces, personal `a_<id>`); package boundaries and the lock file were updated. New actions `catalog.register`, `catalog.read`, `catalog.revoke`.

- **Names and layers.** An agent holding `catalog.register` registers only into its own namespace, `a_` plus a fixed digest of its principal ID (it cannot choose or reuse one). Shared (`toolbox.<name>`) and system (`nimblenewt.<name>`) releases are the owner's: the owner picks an agent's accepted, shared (never private scratch, draft or copy) original record and releases it as its own version, with the author recorded. A personal edit is a new personal version and cannot change a shared release. A name keeps its kind (skill or tool).
- **Versions are immutable.** A version records its manifest, the exact instructional and executable sources (desk record, revision, content digest, author) and a digest over all of it. The same bytes again are a no-op, different bytes under a published version are refused, and versions only increase. Revocation is a separate record by the owner; nothing is deleted. The latest live version is chosen, and a revoked one is refused for a run. `content` reads a source back and refuses it if it is missing or has changed since it was registered. The entry lists every published version and every revoked one, so the latest live version is the highest published number that is not revoked (a revocation falls back to 1.0.5 even when 1.1.0 and 2.0.0 are revoked), and a revocation changes the entry, so a run being recorded at that moment is refused. A new version must be higher than the latest ever published, so a backport (1.0.6 after 1.1.0) is refused by design. A schema may refer only to itself (`$ref` starting with `#`, no `$id`, `$schema` only a json-schema.org dialect).
- **Own accepted original work.** A personal registration goes through `DeskService.executableSource`: another desk's content, a copy of it, a draft and private scratch are refused.
- **Manifest** (checked field by field, each problem names the field): purpose, input and output schemas (JSON objects, 8 KiB, no outside references), entry point and interpreter (tools only), OS and architecture, exact dependency versions, requested access (a list for the owner to read; asking grants nothing), resource limits, cancellation and recovery behaviour.
- **Running.** `authorizeRun` (the dispatcher's call, with the host's facts): a skill cannot run anything; a tool needs a grant naming it in the task (`tool.<name>`, the namespace's underscore written as a hyphen), a revoked version is refused, and a host that cannot satisfy the manifest (OS, architecture, interpreter, dependency versions, CPUs, memory) gets every reason back before anything starts, with nothing recorded. A run that may start is recorded once (who, which exact version and digest, task, project). `usage` shows the records of one project to a reader with `history.read` there, or all to the owner; another project's records are never shown.
- **A release publishes content.** When the owner releases an agent's accepted record as a shared or system version, any holder of `catalog.read` can read its content through the catalog, including from a project they cannot read; the release is the owner's decision to publish it across projects.
- **Usage** is kept per project (a reader with `history.read` there, or the owner, names the project), so a busy project cannot hide another's records or exhaust a scan limit.
- **Limits stated.** The host facts given to `authorizeRun` come from the caller: the dispatcher must take them from the worker's own registration, never from the agent. The catalog records digests and references, not copies of the content: a desk record that is later changed makes `content` refuse rather than serve something else, but the original text is not kept elsewhere; nothing here starts a tool, checks the interpreter on the host itself or enforces the declared limits (the worker and H12); the personal namespace is derived from the principal ID, not from the registry's agent IDs (there is no such ID service yet); who may publish shared names is the owner alone (no delegated publish permission yet).

## A04 notes: path safety, Git-data protection, expected-version writes and the Git bridge (2026-10-22)

A04 is **not** marked implemented until a green full CI run is on record. `packages/worker/src/workspace/` (`names.ts`, `paths.ts`, `bridge.ts`, `git.ts`, `errors.ts`), 23 tests (the Windows junction test runs on the hosted Windows jobs; the Git tests need `git`, which the hosted jobs have). Opus review rounds changed several things, listed here.

**Part 1: names, paths, expected-version writes**

- **Names.** A path is a plain relative path of `/`-separated names: no root, drive, UNC or `\\?\` prefix, no backslash, `.`, `..`, empty part or NUL; no control or `< > : " | ? *`; no trailing dot or space; no Windows device name (`con`, `nul`, `com1`…, also with an extension); on Windows rules also no 8.3-looking name (`PROGRA~1`); at most 255 bytes a name, 64 deep, 1024 in total (240 on Windows, counting the root). `fold` shows a name as a case-insensitive, Unicode-normalising, dot-and-space-dropping filesystem would see it, and `collisions` finds spellings that may be one file.
- **Git's data** is recognised by that fold: `.git` in any spelling (`.GIT`, `.git.`, `.g\u200Bit`, full-width letters, `.git::$INDEX_ALLOCATION`) and its short names (`git~1`, hashed forms such as `GI7EBA~1`). It is recognised before any other check, so a malformed spelling is still reported as Git data. Read, write and delete through the bridge all refuse it. The controller's own reads that need to look inside use a separate internal function (`resolveInside`, not exported from the package).
- **`resolveSafe`** looks at each name on the way without following links, and refuses a symbolic link, a Windows junction (Node reports it as a link), a different spelling of a name already in the directory (collision), a directory where a file is needed, a file that is not plain, and for writing a file with another name elsewhere (hard link). The bridge opens files with `O_NOFOLLOW` and compares what it opened with what it examined; a swap of a parent directory between the check and the use is not detected (the checkout is the agent's own and the shared tree is written only by the bridge).
- **Expected-version writes (`WorkspaceBridge`).** Every change names the version of the file it was based on (`sha256:…`, or `absent`). Check and write are one step under a lock held outside the shared tree. The lock key is the collision key of the path, so every spelling a filesystem treats as one file shares a lock; the lock carries its holder's token and is refreshed while the body runs, a lock untouched for a minute is taken over by renaming it away (only one waiter can), and a holder removes only a lock that still carries its token. New content is written to a new file beside the old one and renamed into place, keeping the old file's mode without setuid or setgid (not its owner or group), never written through the old inode. If the file is no longer at that version nothing is written and the change (path, author, reason, both versions, the whole proposed content) is kept for review; at most 1000 are kept (counted from file names, so a burst can pass the limit by a few) and a further one is refused rather than dropped. A delete works the same way.
- **The owner's decision** (`decide`; the owner API authenticates `decidedBy`): one decision per change, both apply and discard under the change's lock with its state read inside it. Apply carries the change out only if the file is at the version the owner reviewed it against (otherwise `stale` and it stays waiting); discard records the refusal; the record is kept either way, moved from the waiting records to the decided ones. Nothing is merged.

**Part 2: the Git bridge (`GitBridge`)**

- **A checkout has no `.git`.** `createCheckout` makes a repository of its own in a git directory outside the checkout and outside the shared tree (fetched from the shared repository by an explicit local fetch, the only place the file protocol is allowed) and puts the files in the checkout with `--work-tree`. The agent therefore has no `.git` to write, replace, rename, truncate or change the permissions of, and none is mounted into its sandbox. Symlinks in the repository content are not created (`core.symlinks=false`).
- **Nothing the agent or a repository names ever runs.** Every command has a fixed environment (no system or user configuration, an empty home, no prompts, fixed author) and settings on the command line, which win over any configuration file: hooks pointed at an empty directory, no file-system monitor, no external diff, no pager or editor, the SSH command disabled, protocols off, no submodule recursion, no garbage collection. Tests plant hooks in the shared repository and in the checkout's repository, set a hooks path, a monitor and an external diff in its configuration, and put `.gitattributes` (a filter) and `.gitmodules` (a file URL) in the checkout: nothing runs, and removing the hooks-path override makes that test fail.
- **Changes go through the bridge.** `changes` lists what differs from the base commit; before it does, the whole checkout is walked and any name that is Git's own, at any depth and in any spelling, refuses the whole set (git would treat it as another repository and not list its files). `integrate` reads and checks every changed file first (a plain file within the size limit, not a link), and only then writes them one by one with `WorkspaceBridge`, each naming the version of the file at the base commit; a file someone else changed is not overwritten and the agent's change is kept for review; the checkout is never touched. Renames are a delete plus a write; file modes are not carried.
- **Dirty work is never replaced.** `refresh` over a checkout with changed or new files refuses and leaves everything; the caller keeps the work (the quarantine of A03 is for a path whose access was revoked, and is the caller's call).
- **Ignored files are work.** `status` lists files an ignore rule matches too (the ignore file is the agent's own), so `changes`, `integrate` and `refresh` see them: a refresh over ignored work refuses even when the next commit adds a file at that path. A file whose only change is its mode is reported (`modeOnly`) and skipped by `integrate` with a reason, not rewritten; the base version of a binary file is computed from its real bytes; a change or base file over the 8 MiB limit is refused naming the file; `core.longpaths` is on. A write into a directory that does not exist yet makes the directory (each name checked like any other) only when the write is going to happen.
- **Trust boundaries (the caller's duties).** (1) The agent's separate git directory must be outside every sandbox's writable mounts: the dispatcher (H12) adds it to the launcher's protected paths, so a recipe that mounts it is refused, and mounts only the checkout. (2) The shared repository is the owner's to trust: `git fetch` runs `upload-pack` against its configuration and alternates; nothing in that configuration can run a program here (`uploadpack.packObjectsHook` is forced empty, hooks point at an empty directory), but alternates can bring in objects from elsewhere on disk, and the bridge refuses every agent write to `.git`. (3) `safe.directory=*` turns off git's ownership check; that is acceptable only because no repository configuration in play can run a program. (4) The agent must be stopped (frozen or settled) between the walk for Git names, `status`, and the reads in `integrate`; otherwise it can plant a `.git` or swap files after the checks. (5) A takeover of a stale lock checks the lock it moved aside and gives back a fresh one. (6) Directories made for a write stay behind if the write then fails (harmless).
- **Limits stated.** The dispatcher (H12) decides when to create, integrate, refresh or quarantine; nothing here stages or commits in the shared repository (the shared repository's history is advanced by the owner's own process, outside this card), and a commit made in the shared tree outside the bridge is not an agent change. Windows behaviour (junctions, long paths, 8.3 names, case-insensitivity) is checked on the hosted Windows jobs, not on real unattended hardware.

### Owner reads, changed 2026-10-22 (owner decision)

`canRead` now returns true for the installation owner for every desk record (private scratch and drafts included); the ordinary read, `list` and retrieval show them, with no reason and no record. Peers are unchanged: private scratch needs the exact `desk.scratch.read` grant, a draft is its agent's alone. The owner still cannot write to a desk. `InspectionService.inspect` stays as an optional recorded look (and the inspection policy still governs scratch-read grants held by others); the earlier A10 wording "only by inspection" no longer applies.

### A07c and A07b, changed 2026-10-22 (owner decision)

`ProfileSuggestionService` answers are now `switch` (permanent, the default), `trial` (derived for seven days, `TRIAL_DAYS`), `not-now`; a trial that has ended makes the suggestion due again with `keep` (default), `extend` and `revert`. Nothing reverts by itself; an unanswered trial stays derived. A recorded `revert` that was not carried out is finished like a switch. `switchNow` offers `switch`/`trial` outside the three-approval rule (a strict installation only). `ApprovalLoadService.acceptBySwitching` answers a standing-rule proposal by switching the profile (marking it accepted with `via: "profile"`, no grant); the proposal is marked first and the switch is idempotent, so a failure between the two is finished by repeating the call. If the profile was changed by hand in between and the switch then fails, the proposal stays accepted with no grant and no switch, the error goes to the owner, and the proposal can come back after a newer approval because no live grant is linked. A leftover trial record can ask "trial ended?" once after the owner moved to strict and back by hand; any answer is still explicit and harmless.

## A06 notes: the human pre-commit gate and attribution (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/gate/` (`CommitGateService`), `packages/worker/src/workspace/git.ts` (`stage`, `commit`, `markIntegrated`, `changes` rebuilt), 36 controller tests on four backends and 16 worker tests with real git. Opus review round 1 changed the design as described.

- **A review of a tree and a base.** The worker stages the checkout (`stage`: everything git does not ignore is added to the checkout's own index; the tree ID, the checkout's head as base, the staged files and the files an ignore rule keeps out are returned; links, submodule entries and Git-named entries refuse it). The agent asks to commit (`request`): the request names the base, tree, message and files, and becomes an H05 review of the operation `git-commit` with exactly those arguments (the ignored list is shown as the agent's own claim). Asking for a commit also marks the principal as a contributor to every review of that task it is the reviewer of, automatically; the task's reviews are found through an index written with each review (`reviewIdsForTask`), so the cost does not grow with the installation.
- **A changed tree, base, message or file list needs a new review.** `authorize` presents the base, tree and message as they are now; any difference is refused naming it, and the review service's binding refuses a changed operation or changed grants. The permit carries a digest of the file list the reviewer was shown, and the worker's `commit` refuses unless the real difference from the base is that list, so a list that lies about the tree cannot be committed. A refusal does not use the approval up. A successful `authorize` uses it up once; asking again for the same request returns the same permit while it is good (30 minutes), after which a new review is needed. The right to work on the task is checked on every `authorize` and `recordCommit`, also on the external path.
- **The gate is human by default.** Only the owner's approval opens it unless the owner sets the project's gate policy to allow the review policy's reviewers (read just before use; a race with `setPolicy` is possible in a tiny window). An externally verified review (a reference and who verified it) is accepted when the owner records it for this very tree.
- **Identities come from records.** The permit carries the author (the requesting agent, from authentication), the controller as committer, and trailers for the reviewer, how it was reviewed, the developer (who created the task), the executor, the grant that let the agent work, the request and the task. A message with a line that looks like a trailer (Reviewed-by, Approved-by, Acked-by, Tested-by, Co-developed-by, Co-authored-by, Signed-off-by, Author, Developer, Executor, Grant-source, NimbleNewt-…) or one written with letters from another alphabet is refused; the controller's trailers are the last paragraph, the only one git reads as trailers.
- **After the commit.** `recordCommit` stores the commit ID (its parent must be the reviewed base); `recordIntegration` appends branch integrations to the request's history.
- **The checkout's git state (round 1 fix).** `HEAD` is the checkout's own line of commits and `refs/nn/base` is what the shared workspace matched when it started. A second commit builds on the first. `changes` and `integrate` compare the working tree with the base (committed or not, ignored or not, through a throwaway index), so committed work still reaches the shared workspace; `refresh` refuses while the checkout holds commits that are not integrated; `markIntegrated` (everything committed and the shared files now equal to the checkout's) moves the base to `HEAD`, after which a refresh goes through.
- **Limits stated.** The gate covers the commit in the agent's checkout; advancing the shared repository's history goes through the bridge (A04) and the owner's own process, recorded as integrations. `request` takes the file list from its caller, but the worker insists on the real list at commit time. The dispatcher (H12) calls `stage`, `request`, `authorize`, `commit`, `recordCommit`, `integrate`, `markIntegrated`.

## A08 notes: per-agent environments (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/environments/` (`EnvironmentService`), 24 tests on four backends (mutation-checked: skipping the integrity check, letting an agent change anything, resuming on the active instead of the pinned revision, not checking staleness each fail tests). New action `environment.manage`. The card is split: **A08b** (new) enforces the resource limits in the sandbox; A08 holds the rest.

- **A manifest per agent, as numbered immutable revisions.** The common maintained base (name, version, digest), the agent's own package area, its skill library (catalog version IDs, T01), the capabilities the owner approved, the configuration revision at each layer, the limits (CPUs, memory, disk, processes) and the protected extensions with the digest each must have. A change makes a new revision for that agent only (another agent's environment is untouched, tested); a revision is identified by a digest over its manifest.
- **Who changes what.** The owner changes anything. An agent (with `environment.manage`) changes only its own packages and skills on top of a revision the owner made; the base, capabilities, limits, configuration references and protected extensions are the owner's, and an agent never creates its first environment. Staleness and the agent rule are judged inside the command, so a repeat is answered from the record.
- **Limits and headroom.** Limits are checked against the host's capacity with a share (default 10%) kept for the controller: no agent may be given more than 90% of any of the four. Nothing sums limits across agents (overcommit is allowed). **Enforcement in the sandbox is A08b.**
- **Activation verifies the protected extensions.** A revision becomes the agent's active one only if each protected extension has the digest the catalog holds for the live system version of that name and the digest the worker found on disk, no extension is loaded that the manifest does not name, and none is missing; any mismatch activates nothing, names every problem, and the previous revision stays active. Activation and rollback are the owner's.
- **Pins and resume.** Each attempt and checkpoint pins the agent's active revision when it starts (the same pin again returns the same pin), and `resume` returns exactly that revision, with its digest checked, whatever the agent's environment is now. A rollback makes a new revision from an old manifest (history kept) and activates it only if the integrity check passes.
- **Homes.** `authorizeHome` gives an agent's host-side home name (a fixed digest of its ID) to that agent and the owner only; another agent's home is denied. Where homes live and mounting them is the worker's and H12's.
- **Skills are checked.** Every skill in a manifest must be a catalog version that exists, is not revoked and may be used by the person making the change (an agent's own personal entries, shared and system ones with `catalog.read`; `CatalogService.usableVersion`); activation checks again, so a skill revoked later stops the revision. **Pins are the agent's own:** the attempt must exist and be owned by that agent. **What is compared at activation:** `sha256:` plus the digest of the executable source of the latest live system version (`CatalogService.systemDigest`), which is what the manifest names and what the worker reports for the file it loads; a test runs activation against a real catalog entry. A rollback takes the revision the owner saw (so a repeat finishes it) and answers `{ revision, activated }`: when the integrity check fails the new revision exists but is not active and the previous one stays. The capabilities in a manifest record what the owner approved; they are not grants and nothing treats them as permission. Headroom keeps at least one unit on a small host and `reserveFraction` must be from 0 to below 1.
- **Limits stated.** Integrity is checked when a revision is activated; every worker start should check the observed digests against the active revision again (the dispatcher, H12). The catalog's reserved digest is supplied by the caller (`reservedDigest`), as are the digests the worker observed; the worker-side loader (read-only controller-owned extension directory, loader configuration not writable by agents) is H12/T06; a failed rollback activation leaves the new revision made but not active.

## C03 notes: dependency manifests (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/dependencies/` (`DependencyService`), 20 tests on four backends (mutation-checked: freshness-uncertain treated as checkable, an unobserved file read as unchanged, an expired cursor ignored, new directory members ignored each fail tests).

- **What is recorded** (per task, up to 500; the same dependency recorded again replaces its observation): a **file** (hash), a **directory** (the names it held), a **search** (scope, query, the event position it was made at, a hash of its results), a shared **record** or **decision** (hash), a **tool version** (catalog version ID and digest), an **environment** (agent and the revision digest), an **external** resource or an **MCP resource read** (server, protocol revision, URI as the key, hash or ETag, observation time, and how its freshness can be checked) and a **negative lookup** ("there was no such record"). Each kind must say enough to be checked later or the record is refused.
- **`check`** says per entry: `unchanged`, `changed`, `deleted`, `new-members` (a directory with names it did not have), `unknown`, `cursor-expired` (a search made before the oldest readable event) or `freshness-uncertain` (an external or MCP resource recorded without a conditional read or authoritative query). Records, decisions, tool versions (including revocation), environments and negative lookups of desk records and catalog entries are judged from the controller's own state; files, directories, searches, external resources and other negative lookups need a current observation from the worker, and with none the answer is `unknown`, never `unchanged`.
- **Who.** The agent working the task (or the owner) records; those who may work the task, read the project's histories, or the owner read the manifest and run `check`.
- **Limits stated.** The service holds what was observed and what is observed now; reading files, listing directories, repeating searches and conditional reads are the worker's and H12's. "Non-Git inputs" are files like any other. A directory's recorded members are capped at 200 names. MCP calls themselves are T03; this card records what they read.

## T03 notes: approved MCP client connections (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/mcp/` (`McpService`), 24 tests on four backends (mutation-checked: a changed capability being called, the audience ignored, http allowed, internal hosts allowed, the lifecycle gate not checked each fail tests). New action `mcp.use`. This card is the registry, approval and call pinning; the client that talks to a server (the SDK, the transport) and the launch itself are the worker's and H12's.

- **Approval by the owner (the trusted administrator).** A server is registered by name with its mode (local: stdio; remote: streamable HTTP), a protocol revision and SDK build from the supported list (`MCP_SUPPORT`: revisions 2025-06-18 and 2025-03-26, SDK `@modelcontextprotocol/sdk` 1.12.0), the projects that may use it (the audience) and the tools, resources and prompts that may be used at all. A server not registered, revoked, or not allowed for a project is denied.
- **Local servers** start through the sandbox launcher with a plan (`launchPlan`): an absolute program path, arguments, only the listed variables (a name rule that refuses `LD_`/`DYLD_`/`LDR_`, and values that look like secrets) and the names of secrets to inject from the secret store (F09); `inheritEnvironment` is always false. **Remote servers** need an https address (no credentials in the URL, no loopback, private, link-local or internal host or bare name), a credential that is the name of a secret (a value that looks like a credential is refused) and at least one narrow scope, never a wildcard.
- **Catalog snapshots.** What a server offers is reported by the host as numbered snapshots of names with digests of each description and schema (the text itself is not kept as an instruction). The owner reads what changed (`pendingChanges`: added, removed, changed) and accepts a revision. A capability is callable only while it is allowed, present in the accepted snapshot, still offered, and unchanged since: a description or schema that changed after approval is not called until the owner accepts the change.
- **A call** is checked in this order: the lifecycle gate (a callback; the lifecycle cards supply it), the server's approval, the audience, the grants (`mcp.use` for the server and `attempt.work` on the task), the allowed list, the accepted catalog. It is recorded, per task, with the server, the protocol revision and the accepted catalog revision it was pinned to; `recordOutcome` stores ok, error or uncertain (a call whose answer was lost, or that never reported back, is shown as uncertain). A resource read that ends well enters the task's dependency manifest (C03) with server, protocol, URI, hash or ETag and how its freshness can be checked. `activity` lists a task's calls (server, capability, outcome, uncertainty, revisions, time; no arguments) to those who may work it, read the project's histories, or the owner.
- **Results are data.** `frameResult` marks output as untrusted data from an outside server, cuts it to 20,000 characters, removes credentials and flags phrases that look like orders to the model; nothing in the controller reads a result as a command, and a changed description cannot widen what is allowed.
- **Address checks.** `internalHost` judges the form of the name: IPv4 and IPv6 literals (loopback, private, carrier-grade, link-local, site-local, documentation, benchmarking, multicast and broadcast, and the IPv6 forms that carry an IPv4 address: mapped, compatible, NAT64), names ending in `.localhost`, `.local` or `.internal` (also with a trailing dot) and bare names. The URL parser has already turned decimal, octal and hex IPv4 spellings into dotted form. A public name that resolves to a private address (DNS rebinding) must be refused at connect time, after resolution, by the worker that makes the connection: that clause is on card H12.
- **Further limits stated.** Any host or service principal may report a catalog for any server (the owner sees who reported the latest in `pendingChanges`); a revoked server's name cannot be registered again (re-approval is not built); launch variables such as `NODE_OPTIONS`, `PYTHONPATH` or `PATH` change what code runs and are the owner's to set (a later settings view should warn); `activity` is capped at 2,000 calls, sorted by time. A repeat of a read's outcome runs the dependency step again, which is idempotent.
- **Limits stated.** Nothing here speaks MCP or starts a process; the host reports snapshots (`recordCatalog` takes a host, service or the owner); the lifecycle gate is a callback until the lifecycle cards exist; protected tools do not use this service at all, so they work with no server registered (a call to an unregistered server is simply denied).

## H06 notes: event subscriptions and gap recovery (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/events/` (`EventService`), 28 tests on four backends (mutation-checked: events waking despite a hold or cooldown, loops not suppressed, no backpressure, a source's repeats accepted, gaps not reported each fail tests). New action `events.publish`.

- **Streams and sequence.** A stream is `project:<id>` or `task:<id>`. Producers (the controller's own services, hosts, the owner, or an agent holding `events.publish` in the project) publish `{kind, ref, key, causedBy?, source?}`; each event gets the stream's next sequence number. A repeated publish command is the same event.
- **Subscriptions.** A subscriber who may read the project subscribes from now on (or from the start) with the kinds it wants, a cooldown and a batch size. `pull` returns the events after its cursor in order, only its kinds, and the latest of events that share a key (with a count of those folded in); reading changes nothing, and `acknowledge` moves the cursor forward only (an old or repeated acknowledgement does nothing).
- **Gaps.** The result says `reconcile: true`, with reasons, when an event is missing from the sequence, when a source's own numbering skipped (the gap is kept until the owner, a host or a service says it was reconciled), and when the cursor is older than the retention floor (the cursor jumps to the floor). A source's repeated or late number is ignored as a duplicate. The subscriber then reconciles from the source of truth; nothing is guessed.
- **Holds, cooldowns, loops, pressure.** An event never lifts a manual hold: while the stream's scope is held (a callback the pause service supplies) the events wait and `wake` is false; each subscription has a cooldown after a wake (`retryAt` says when). Every event records its causation chain; an event whose kind and record already appear in its own ancestry, or whose chain is 20 deep, is stored as suppressed and never delivered. A publisher is told to wait (`accepted: false`, retry after 5 seconds) when the slowest active *critical* subscriber (owner, host or service; recorded in a per-stream watcher index) is 1000 events behind. Agent and client subscribers never slow producers: one that falls more than 1000 behind is told to reconcile and its cursor jumps forward. `cancel` ends a subscription (its subscriber or the owner). A cause must be an event of the same stream, otherwise it answers as missing. A `pull` reads at most 2000 events and `through` marks where it stopped. Everything judged against stream state (backpressure, source repeats, causes) happens inside the command, so a repeat of a recorded publish returns its recorded number.
- **Limits stated.** The sequence head is one record per stream, so publishers to one stream at the same moment retry (eight times) and can fail with a stale-revision error under heavy contention; retention only moves a floor (no event is deleted); a `task:` stream's project is not resolved (publishing and subscribing to a `task:` stream need the owner, a host or a service, or an agent with a grant covering every project); a source's late number (below the last seen) is reported `late: true` and is not delivered, since the recorded gap already makes subscribers reconcile; idle subscriptions do not expire yet, so an abandoned critical watcher (a crashed host) holds producers back until the owner cancels it, and nothing yet shows the owner which watcher that is; the pause service that supplies the hold callback and the dispatcher that pulls are later cards.

## T04 notes: the state API through MCP (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/mcp/state-adapter.ts` (`McpStateAdapter`), 4 tests (one per backend, mutation-checked: any protocol revision accepted, hidden and missing resources told apart, batches accepted each fail).

- **Same door.** Each JSON-RPC message carries the caller's credential and goes to `StateApi.handle`, so authentication, scoping, privacy, validation and idempotency are the native ones; the adapter decides nothing. Tools (`project_get`, `task_transition`, …) and their schemas are generated from the operation table; commands require a `commandId` the client keeps across a reconnect (a repeat is answered from the record, a reused key with a different command is refused), queries take none. Errors come back as tool results with `isError` and the native code.
- **Resources.** `nimblenewt://project/<id>` and `nimblenewt://task/<id>`, listed from `project.list` (paged by cursor). A task or project the caller may not read answers exactly as one that does not exist.
- **Protocol.** `initialize` accepts only the revisions in `MCP_SUPPORT` and otherwise says which are supported. Notifications get no reply; batches are refused; unknown methods are errors.
- **Choices.** An unsupported protocol revision is an error that lists the supported ones (the card's "fails clearly"), not a counter-offer. `initialize`, `ping`, `tools/list`, templates and `prompts/list` answer without a credential (they show only the public operation table) and the adapter keeps no session, so tools can be called without initializing.
- **Limits stated.** No transport (HTTP or stdio framing, session handling) and no subscriptions or `listChanged`; resource listing covers projects only; the credential is passed to `handle` by the transport, which does not exist yet.

## C04 notes: safe-boundary continuation capture (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/continuation/` (`ContinuationService`), 4 tests (one per backend, mutation-checked: a failed durability check reported complete, a turn in progress accepted, secrets kept, an incomplete capture resumed, a changed conversation identity accepted each fail). New id prefixes `cnt` (continuation) and `cnr` (resume).

- **Capture.** A host, service, the owner or an agent working on the task hands over: the conversation identity, the boundary (`turn-complete`, `timeout`, `manual`, `shutdown`; a turn in progress is refused), the adapter's checkpoint reference (format, conversation, position, digest, runtime), the result of the host's durability check, artifacts, decisions, pending effects, budget and next step, the manifest (summary lineage, source ranges, compression state, branches, message cursor, routing and environment revisions, adapter name and version), the tools exposed that a checkpoint cannot reproduce (kept by name and reason), the scratch records the work depends on, and credential *names*. Every string anywhere is checked and a credential-like one refuses the capture, naming the place.
- **Whose word.** A capture names the run's agent (an agent captures only for itself). Every scratch record it lists must be that agent's own and in the task's project, checked at capture and again when the recovery service reads (a record that no longer matches is skipped). `durable.verified: true` is accepted only from the owner, a host or a service; an agent's capture is always recorded incomplete ("durability was not verified by the host"), so a host confirms by capturing after its check.
- **Complete means verified.** A capture is `complete` only when the durability check passed; otherwise it is stored as `incomplete` with the problem (a full disk, a failed read-back), never offered for resuming and never replaces the latest complete one. One head record per task holds the next number, the fixed identity and the latest complete capture.
- **Resume.** After a restart or with a different model, `resume` returns the same conversation identity and the latest complete capture (or a named complete one), and records the resume with the model and whether it changed. A repeat of the command is the same resume.
- **Recovery reads.** The recovery service reads the scratch records a capture points to only through `InspectionService.recoveryRead`, one look per record, each of which the agent can see in its inspections.
- **Hosts should know.** `capture` does not retry when another capture lands at the same moment (the caller repeats it). A recovery read of the same command ID is a replay and returns no content (the inspection service treats a second reading as a new look), so a retry after a failure partway needs a new command ID and records already read are not returned again. `recoveryScratch` refuses an incomplete continuation.
- **Limits stated.** The service trusts the host's word that the durability check passed and that the checkpoint reference is the adapter's (it holds no content and the digest is the adapter's); it does not itself call the adapter, write files or measure disk space; artifact, decision and effect IDs are format-checked, not looked up; capturing does not itself stop the run (timeouts and shutdown belong to the lifecycle cards); old captures are not pruned.

## A08b notes: per-agent resource limits in the sandbox (2026-10-22)

`packages/worker/src/sandbox/limits.ts` (plan) and `launcher.ts` (`LaunchRequest.limits`, `requireEnforced`; `LauncherConfig.headroom`, `limitCapabilities`; `LaunchRecord.limits`). 12 tests in `limits.test.ts`: the plan for every capability combination and other systems, and real confined processes (memory, file size, process count, CPU affinity); mutation-checked by loosening each limit.

- **What is applied (Linux).** With a working user systemd scope (found by really trying `systemd-run --user --scope` at creation): memory (`MemoryMax`, swap off), process and thread count (`TasksMax`) and CPU time (`CPUQuota`) are enforced by the cgroup, reported `full`. Without one: memory falls back to an address-space cap (`partial`: per process, not the group), CPUs to an affinity mask (`full`), and the process count is `none` (the OS limit counts all of the service user's processes, so it would also stop the service). Disk is only a per-file size cap (`partial`): a total needs a filesystem quota, which a worker does not own. macOS and Windows have nothing wired in yet and report every limit `none`.
- **Never silent.** Each launch record lists every limit asked for with `enforced: full | partial | none`, what enforced it and what it does not cover. `requireEnforced` refuses the launch, before anything is recorded or run, unless every limit is `full`.
- **Headroom.** Limits are checked against the machine less a reserve for the controller (default 1 core and 1 GiB, configurable); a request for more than is left is refused.
- **Details worth knowing.** Newer systemd expands `$NAME` in a scope's command line and older ones (Ubuntu 24.04's 255, found by the first CI run) pass it through as written, so the capability probe runs a printf through a scope and records whether dollar signs must be written `$$`; the wrapper doubles them only then; an inconclusive probe (or a host without /usr/bin/printf) means the cgroup scope is not used and the fallbacks apply. The wrapper gets only `XDG_RUNTIME_DIR` and `DBUS_SESSION_BUS_ADDRESS` from the service's environment; the agent's environment is still cleared by the sandbox.
- **Known weaknesses (from review).** In trusted-host mode the session bus address reaches the command, so a process could start a unit outside its scope and escape the limits; trusted-host already has the service user's full access, but the report says `full`. The affinity mask always uses cores 0 to n-1, so agents share the first cores and the reserved core is not chosen. The user scope lives in the user manager, not the worker service's cgroup; cleanup relies on `--collect` and the process ending (the sandboxed child still dies with bwrap).
- **Limits stated.** Disk totals, macOS and Windows enforcement (Job Objects, launchd limits) are not built and are reported as such; a CI container without a user systemd uses the fallbacks and the process-count fixture checks that `none` is on the record instead; cgroup limits apply to the whole group the sandbox starts, not to each process in it; the environment manifest's limits (A08) are not yet connected to this request (the environment service supplies the numbers; wiring them into a launch belongs to the worker integration card).

## C07 notes: context budgets and stall-proof helpers (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/context/budget.ts`, 10 tests (`budget.test.ts`; pure functions, no storage). Split on 2026-10-22: this card holds requirements 1, 2, 3, 5, 6, 7 and 8 of context-window-stalls.md as functions the dispatcher will call; the Pi-side and dispatch wiring (bounded summariser through `beforeCompact`, explicit stream timeouts, measuring the first call against the provider's reported usage, tool limits applied inside Pi and MCP tools, fallback-route rebuild, compressor registration, lineage and readable-by-all-sources) is the new card **C07b**.

- **Budgets.** `contextBudget(window)`: startup at most 25%, startup plus tool output plus reply reserve 60%, resume 60%, one tool result about 4% of the window in characters (16,000 on a 131,072 window), three characters per token; all configurable and validated (the shares are the Paperclip team's estimates and must be measured).
- **Startup builder.** Human words, permissions, the goal and the newest six items are always whole; older items are cut to a 400-character head and 300-character tail oldest first, then dropped to a one-line reference if still too large; every cut or dropped item is returned in full with a digest for the caller to archive and link. If what must stay whole does not fit, the result is blocked with the numbers and no prompt is returned. The caller supplies the measured size of everything else in the first call.
- **Resume guard, tool output, inquiry plan, failure class, hand-off.** `resumeDecision` (over 60% means fresh start from the continuation record), `limitToolOutput`/`page` (head or tail kept, remainder returned for archiving and paging, images and other non-text results counted at a fixed cost and dropped with a note when they do not fit), `inquiryBranchPlan` (latest boundary, else latest compaction plus retrieval, else fresh plus retrieval, with a stated reason), `classifyFailure` (`context_overflow` for the Hermes/Pi/provider messages), `handoffFromFacts` (ledger, artifacts, checkpoint and last state, credentials redacted, bounded).
- **Limits stated.** Token counts are estimates from characters until the dispatcher measures real usage; nothing here is wired to a model, a dispatcher or Pi (C07b and H12); archiving the cut text and paging the remainder are the caller's job (an artifact write); the 60% working limit (startup plus tool output plus reply reserve) is validated here and enforced at dispatch by C07b; `classifyFailure` is pattern matching on messages (rate-limit and configuration lookalikes are excluded), to be replaced by the adapter's typed reason where Pi gives one.

## R02 notes: provider failure classification, bounded fallback and cumulative budgets (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/routing/fallback.ts` (`FallbackService`, `classifyProviderFailure`), 29 tests on four backends (mutation-checked: an uncertain effect ignored, unbounded attempts, hopeless failures retried elsewhere, the same provider tried again after a credit or authentication failure, the budget ignored each fail). New id prefix `mcl` (model call).

- **A call.** The dispatcher (owner, host or service) `begin`s a call for a task with a route request and gets a step: a route, a visible wait, or failed. The route is the first eligible one in the routing table *as it is now* (R01 `select`), skipping any already tried. `failed` classifies the failure (credit, rate-limit, outage, authentication, context overflow, invalid request, unknown; messages are redacted and cut to 300 characters before they are kept), and `succeeded` records the usage. A repeat of any report is the recorded answer and charges once.
- **Bounded fallback.** Credit, rate-limit and outage failures move on to the next eligible route; authentication and credit failures also skip the rest of that provider for this call. A context overflow, an invalid request or an unknown failure is not tried elsewhere (the call fails and says why; overflow points to C07). At most four attempts in all.
- **Visible wait.** With nothing eligible left the call waits, with the reason, the list of why each route was skipped, and the time the earliest cooldown ends (60 s after a rate limit, 5 min after an outage). `resume` tries again: cooldowns passed, the table changed (an exclusion lifted, a route added) or the budget raised.
- **No repeated external effect.** The caller reports the effects of the failed attempt. If any is `uncertain` the call waits for reconciliation instead of trying another route (`resume` with the reconciled state continues), and every route step lists the effects that must not be done again (`doNotRepeat`: intended and confirmed ones), so the dispatcher reuses the ledger's keys (S05) and the fallback route never does them a second time.
- **Cumulative budgets.** Spend (cost in millionths of the currency, tokens, calls) is added up per task across every attempt, including failed ones that report usage. The owner can cap cost and/or tokens per task; a task at or over its cap does not begin a call (it waits, saying so). Project-level and delegated budgets, the request-more-budget flow and local compute time belong to R05.
- **Review fixes.** Spend is kept per call (an entry of the task's `msp-` records that only that call writes) and summed, so calls of one task reporting at once never collide; the attempt that just failed counts against the budget for the next route; a fifth attempt never starts, even after an uncertain effect is reconciled; a credit or authentication block lasts for the call, and only the owner's `resume` (after fixing billing) clears it; `step` is for dispatchers only; a failed call keeps its reason under `ended`, not `waiting`. A quota 'per minute' or a bare 429 is a rate limit (not credit); a bare 403 is not treated as an authentication failure.
- **Soft cap, scan cost.** Two calls of one task starting together can both pass the budget check, so the cap can be exceeded by up to one call's usage per call in flight; `spend()` reads every call's entry of the task on each decision, so a very long-lived task would want a periodically folded total.
- **Limits stated.** Classification is pattern matching on messages and status codes until providers give typed reasons; cooldowns are per call (a shared provider-health memory across calls is not built, so ten calls may each try a rate-limited route once); the effect states are reported by the dispatcher, not read from the S05 ledger here; the budget check happens at `begin` and `resume`, so one call may overspend by its own cost.

## F04i notes: refuse unsupported filesystems unless explicitly overridden (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/environment/filesystem.ts` (detection and check), the list in `supported.ts` (`SUPPORTED_ENVIRONMENT.filesystems`, `NETWORK_FILESYSTEMS`), the wiring in `api/start.ts` (`dataDirectory`, `workspaceRoots`, `filesystem` options; `Controller.filesystems`). 15 tests in `filesystem.test.ts` (including the audit record on every storage backend) and 5 in `api/start.test.ts`; mutation-checked (network flag ignored, a type added to the list, the mount table's longest match reversed, the file system problems dropped from the decision).

- **The list.** Linux ext4, xfs, btrfs; macOS APFS; Windows NTFS, in the same file as the F04h list. A type is compared as the system names it, lower case. Anything else is a problem: tmpfs, overlay, ext2/3, ZFS, FAT, exFAT, ReFS, a FUSE driver (`fuseblk` too), and every network type. Case-insensitive and normalising APFS and NTFS are supported; the workspace bridge folds names itself.
- **Found by probing.** Linux reads `/proc/self/mountinfo` and takes the longest mount point holding the path (statfs magic numbers if the table is unreadable; `0xef53` is ext2, ext3 and ext4 alike, so that fallback never matches and refuses). macOS parses `/sbin/mount` (a mount without the `local` flag is a network mount). Windows asks PowerShell's `DriveInfo` for the drive's format and type, and treats a UNC path as network. A directory that does not exist yet is judged by its nearest existing parent. If the file system cannot be found the controller refuses ("unknown"), it never guesses. Nothing here reads a distribution name. Detection is injectable (`FilesystemDetector`) and the mount-table parsers are pure.
- **One decision, one switch.** `decideStartup` (new, in `check.ts`; `startupCheck` now calls it) takes every problem (operating system, Node.js, file systems, prerequisites), so one refusal lists them all and names `--allow-unsupported-environment` / `NIMBLENEWT_ALLOW_UNSUPPORTED_ENVIRONMENT` once, and says how to verify (the QA script with the data directory and workspaces on that file system). The override prints the notice, is audited once per epoch (`environment.override`) and changes nothing else. `Problem.item` is now a string and `howToTest` optional.
- **Which paths.** The data directory is the directory of the SQLite file, or `StartOptions.dataDirectory` (a PostgreSQL controller has none unless given one: it has no local database files). Workspaces: `StartOptions.workspaceRoots`; a network file system under a workspace is refused too because it is not on the list.
- **Naming probe.** Where the directory exists, one file is created and removed in a fresh `.nn-fs-probe-*` directory to find out whether names fold by case or Unicode form (`FilesystemFacts.caseInsensitive`, `normalizing`, returned in `Controller.filesystems`, information only). The existing bridge tests (`workspace.test.ts`: `fold`, `collisions`, refusal of another spelling of an existing file) are what cover the folding itself.
- **Limits.** Supported does not mean tested: xfs and btrfs are on the list by the owner's decision and CI exercises only ext4, APFS and NTFS (the GitHub Windows runner's work drive may be ReFS or NTFS; check the first CI run). A mounted folder that crosses volumes on Windows is judged by its drive. A FUSE mount that hides a local store is refused as unsupported. Nobody calls this for workspaces yet: the controller has no workspace registry, so the worker-side start-up and U01 must pass `workspaceRoots`. The SQLite adapter's own `assertLocalFilesystem` (UNC paths and Linux network mounts) is still called by `SqliteStorage.open`; it now takes `allowNetworkFilesystem`, which start-up sets from the override (like `allowUnsupportedServer` for PostgreSQL), so the override works end to end (tested with a UNC path with and without it). Without the flag the adapter still refuses on its own. The Windows work drive of the CI runner must report NTFS (not ReFS) or the real detector test cell will refuse; like every real probe here (PowerShell `DriveInfo`, `/sbin/mount`, `sandbox-exec`), it is proven only by the first CI run.
- **Existing tests.** `api/start.test.ts` now passes a simulated ext4 detector in every call, because the temporary directory is tmpfs on some machines.

## F04j notes: check required tools and libraries instead of distribution names (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/environment/prerequisites.ts` (probes and check), the list in `supported.ts` (`REQUIRED_PREREQUISITES`), `storage/sqlite/library.ts` and `storage/postgres/library.ts` (the database library probes; only the storage folders may touch a driver), the wiring in `api/start.ts` (`features`, `probes`, `distribution`; `Controller.prerequisites`). 14 tests in `prerequisites.test.ts` (audit record on every backend) and 2 in `api/start.test.ts`; mutation-checked (the too-old comparison removed, the hard-stop ignoring the switch, the skip of a dependent probe, the host missing from the audit record, features ignored).

- **The list.** git (minimum 2.38.1, derived from the flags the workspace bridge sets, for the owner to confirm), bubblewrap and the kernel's unprivileged user namespaces (Linux sandbox), `sandbox-exec` (macOS sandbox), a systemd user session (limits), the SQLite library, the PostgreSQL client library. Windows has no sandbox backend yet (A03b), so nothing is asked for there. Node is not in this list: its version stays in F04h's `SUPPORTED_ENVIRONMENT.node` and `checkEnvironment` (a test pins that no second Node entry exists).
- **Probing.** Each probe runs the thing: `git --version`, `bwrap --version`, then bubblewrap with the launcher's own flags starting a confined `sh -c "exit 0"` (so an old bubblewrap or a kernel or container that forbids user namespaces is found at start-up, not at the first launch), `systemd-run --user --scope`, opening an in-memory `node:sqlite` database, loading `pg`. A probe that throws, or that nobody supplied, counts as missing. The user-namespace probe is skipped when bubblewrap is missing (one cause, one problem). The distribution (`/etc/os-release` `PRETTY_NAME`, control characters removed) is read only to write one `Host:` line in refusals and notices and into the audit record of an override (`detail.host`); a test pins that no check reads it and that the code names no distribution or package manager. A supported start with no problems stays silent and writes no record, so the distribution is audited only when an override is.
- **Hard versus overridable.** A missing prerequisite cannot be overridden (the refusal says so on its line and does not offer the switch for it; with other, overridable problems the message offers the switch for those only, and the hard ones still stop the start). A version below the minimum can be overridden with the same F04h switch, and the message says what that means. The systemd user session is the one missing item that can be overridden (decision below).
- **Which are asked.** Always `start` (git) and the storage in use (`sqlite` or `postgres`). The sandbox and limits tools are required at start only when the controller is told it runs them locally (`StartOptions.features: ["sandbox", "limits"]`, default none): a controller that only schedules remote workers does not need bubblewrap, and the worker's own launcher already refuses to advertise capacity without its tools (A03, A08b). The caller (U01, the worker start-up) must pass `features`.
- **Limits.** The probes duplicate small parts of the launcher's health checks because the controller package cannot import the worker's; a change to the launcher's flags needs the same change in `realProbes`. Only git has a version floor; bubblewrap, systemd and SQLite versions are not checked (the real start of a confined process is the test). The macOS and Windows probes are written but run only on those systems' CI cells; the macOS probe's `sandbox-exec -n no-network` form is untested here. Missing prerequisites on Windows other than git and the libraries are not asked for until A03b. A git that prints no parseable version passes silently (no floor can be applied). The bubblewrap flag subset shared by `realProbes` and the launcher is `BUBBLEWRAP_FLAGS`; a test pins it to `packages/worker/src/sandbox/linux.ts`. When the switch is given but a hard problem remains, the refusal says the switch was given and does not cover those items.

### Lead decisions (F04i, F04j, 2026-10-22)
Every start writes the host line (system, architecture, distribution) once per epoch as an `environment.host` audit record, with no console output, in addition to the refusal and notice lines; a missing systemd user session stays overridable; sandbox and limits tools are required only when `StartOptions.features` names them; a network or unsupported file system under a workspace root is refused too; git floor 2.38.1 confirmed; the override reaches the SQLite adapter (`allowNetworkFilesystem`). (The earlier statement that a supported start writes no record applies to `environment.override` only.)

### Decisions I made (F04i, F04j)
Network and unsupported file systems under a workspace are refused as well as under the data directory; the systemd user session is the only missing item that the override covers (weaker limits exist and the launch record reports them); the sandbox and limits tools are required at start only through `features`; git floor 2.38.1.

## H06b notes: idle expiry of event subscriptions (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/events/events.ts` (`EventService`: `configureIdle`, `heartbeat`, `status`, `resume`, `expireIdle`, and `noticeThroughNotifications`), 14 tests in `events.test.ts` (the 9 of H06 unchanged and passing, 5 added here) on four backends, 56 runs. Mutation-checked: idleness never detected, an acknowledgement or heartbeat not counting as activity, an idle watcher still holding producers, a subscriber's own time not capped by the owner's, an expiry that moves the cursor, no owed Notice, resume of a cancelled subscription, and an agent allowed to sweep each fail tests. New audit types `events.expire`, `events.noticed`, `events.resume`, `events.heartbeat`, `events.idle-configure`.

- **What counts as activity.** Creating the subscription, `acknowledge` (even an old or repeated position), `heartbeat` (for a host that is busy with a long batch) and `resume`. `pull` stays read-only and is not activity.
- **The time.** The owner sets one idle time (`configureIdle`, owner only, 10 s to 7 days; default 900 s) that is a ceiling: a subscriber may ask for less when it subscribes (`idleSeconds`) and never more. The setting is read at each check, so a change counts from the next one. Time comes from the runner's injected clock; nothing sleeps.
- **Who expires it.** (1) `expireIdle`, the scheduler's call (owner or service principal only), pages through every stream that has had an event and every subscription listed for it (a new per-stream `sbl-` index, written by `subscribe`), expiring at most 100 per call (`more: true` says to call again). (2) Every `publish` already read the critical watchers to judge back-pressure; a watcher idle past its time is now left out of that count, so a crashed host stops holding producers back at the idle time even if no sweep has run, and the publish then expires it for the record. So the bound is the idle time, not the idle time plus a sweep interval.
- **What expiry does.** The subscription becomes `expired`, keeps its cursor (also kept as `expiredCursor`), and stops being counted for back-pressure. The audit entry `events.expire` (the activity record) names the subscriber, stream, cursor, last activity, idle time, whether it was critical and how many events were waiting. The expiry command ID names the activity it expires after, so a later second expiry is a separate record and a repeat sweep never doubles one. It re-checks idleness inside the command under the record's revision, so an acknowledgement that lands first wins and the sweep skips it.
- **After expiry.** `pull`, `acknowledge` and `heartbeat` on an expired subscription fail with `invalid_transition` ("resume it"); `status` (subscriber or owner) shows state, cursor and `expiredCursor`. `resume` (subscriber only) reactivates it with the same cursor: every event after the last acknowledged one is served, none before it is repeated; if the cursor has fallen behind retention or more than the queue bound, the next `pull` says `reconcile` with the same reasons as H06. `cancel` by the subscriber or owner works at any time in any state and is final (a cancelled subscription cannot be resumed or expired).
- **Notice.** U11b (the Needs-you view) is not in code. The notification record that exists is U11's `NotificationService`, so `noticeThroughNotifications(notifications)` is the `notify` option: it files a `maintenance` item through `emitFromController`, pointing at `subscription/<id>`, with the project, the cursor and the number of events left unhandled in the inbox-only detail text (so it joins the digest). "Work was affected" is defined as: events were waiting for it (`pending > 0`); an expiry with nothing waiting is recorded in the audit only. A failed delivery leaves `noticePending` on the subscription and the next `expireIdle` retries it (command IDs make a retry a repeat); the expiry itself never fails because a Notice did. When U11b exists, its adapter replaces the callback; nothing else changes.
- **Not here / limits.** Nothing calls `expireIdle` on a schedule yet (the dispatcher or an S08 timer owns that); subscriptions on a stream that has never had an event are not swept (no head record to find them by; they hold nothing back); only publishing reaps inline, so an idle agent subscriber (not critical, never slows producers) waits for the sweep; an owner cannot set a time for one subscription, only the ceiling; a host that returns after its expiry must call `status` or handle `invalid_transition` and `resume` (no automatic resume); 

**H06b review fixes and decisions.** Tests are now 17 per backend (68 runs). (1) The Notice's command ID is keyed by the expiry (`expiredAt`), not the cursor: a host that expires, resumes without acknowledging and expires again is reported both times (U11 treats a repeated command ID as a replay). (2) A failing notifier never stops a sweep: `expireIdle` counts `noticeFailed`, leaves `noticePending` set and carries on, and returns `{ expired, noticed, noticeFailed, more }`. (3) `publish` only expires (never calls the notifier), so a slow channel cannot slow a producer; the Notice goes out at the next sweep. (4) The sweep stops reading at its 100-expiry limit (`more: true`). (5) The idle re-check inside the expire command is load-bearing: a test lands an acknowledgement between the sweep's read and the command's own read and shows the subscription stays active. (6) Lead decisions: an expiry of a critical (host or service) subscription always files a Notice, even with nothing pending (a host disappeared); a non-critical one only when events were pending; sweeping stays limited to owner and service principals; the owner's idle setting stays ceiling-only.
- **Index limits.** There is no delete change, so a cancelled subscription keeps its `sbl-` entry and is skipped (still read) by the sweep. Subscriptions created before H06b have no `sbl-` entry: non-critical ones are never swept; critical ones are expired only by `publish` (and their Notice is then never sent, as only the sweep sends it for entries it can see). No backfill was written.

## C01b notes: the shared working-notes tier (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/context/notes.ts` (`WorkingNoteService`), `packages/controller/src/security/credential-patterns.ts`, 11 tests per backend (`notes.test.ts`, four backends, 44 runs). A second desk tier beside private scratch: entries an agent writes on purpose for its project while work is not yet in the codebase (status, decisions with their reason, measurements, hand-off state). Notes are their own records (`working-note`, with `working-note-version`, `-promotion` and `-deletion` records), not desk records, so desk drafts and scratch rules are untouched.

- **Who reads.** The note's agent, the owner, principals holding `desk.read` in the note's project (the project's members), and a meeting chair there. No chair exists yet, so the stand-in is the exact grant `notes.chair.read` (a new action in `ACTIONS`); M05b should give a chair that grant (or replace the check with the real chair test). A private note is read by its agent and the owner only (the `desk.scratch.read` grant does not open notes). Another project reads a shared note only for a project of its own named as `viaProject`, where it has read access and the owner has opened an A10 path from the note's project; a path does not carry private notes, versions of private notes or deleted notes. An unreadable note reads like a missing one (`forbidden`; the owner gets `not_found` for a missing one).
- **Who writes.** Only the note's agent, with `desk.write` now (revoking it ends revising; taking a note back to private stays possible, sharing again needs the grant). Peers, supervisors with `*` and the owner cannot revise an agent's note; the owner uses `correct`.
- **Versions.** Each write, revision, visibility change and correction creates a version record (`<note>.<n>`) with note revision, author, time, visibility and content; a read returns the note with its ID and revision, `versions` returns the history (`noteRevision` is the note's revision; the view's own `revision` is the version record's). A correction by the owner has the owner as author and `correction: true`.
- **Bounds.** Title 200, body 8,000, reason 1,000 characters; at most 50 revisions per note (then the agent writes a new note); a decision note must give a reason. There is no cap on the number of notes per agent yet.
- **Credential patterns.** `CREDENTIAL_PATTERNS` and `findCredentialShapes` are the reusable list. `redact.ts` now takes its patterns from it, so redaction and refusal share one list; the list was extended with Google API keys, Stripe keys and npm tokens (redaction therefore also catches those now). A refusal names the kind of shape, never the text. Limits: shapes only, so a split, encoded or paraphrased secret passes, and a sentence such as `token: refreshed` is refused (the named-assignment pattern).
- **Mark private, promote.** `setVisibility` flips shared/private as a new revision. `promote` records a request (`working-note-promotion`: note, revision, content digest, target `codebase` or `docs`, repository-relative path, reason, optional `reviewId`) and changes nothing else. It does not call the review service: the agent makes the review request through the normal path and may pass its ID (checked: a review the agent made in the same project); nothing yet links a decided review back to the promotion record, and nothing writes the file. A later card (or the review path itself) must act on the record.
- **Not woken.** Reads and listings only read stored records: they message nobody, take no lock on the author and write nothing, so notes stay readable while the author is inside a long-running command. The test shows reads do not advance the note and the author's next write still succeeds; there is no author-process model in the code yet to hold "busy" more literally.
- **Owner.** Reads everything (private and deleted markers), corrects, and deletes. Deletion empties the note and every version (a marker with author, times and revision remains), and writes a deletion record (who, why, when, final revision, a digest of the removed content, never the content). `deletions` lists them for the owner. Deleted notes vanish from listings and reads for everyone but the owner.
- **Not done.** The chair read is tested only with the stand-in grant (M05b adds the real test); no API surface or MCP tool exposes the service yet; the owner's edits are not shown to the author beyond the version history.
- **Review fixes (same day).** An agent can always mark a note private again, even at the 50-revision cap (revision 51; deletion stays bounded by the version count). Owner corrections and marking private have a hard ceiling of 60 revisions (50 + 10) beyond which the owner is told to delete the note or have the agent start a new one; delete and `versions` are bounded by it. `versions` shows peers only versions written while the note was shared; the agent and owner see all (the current text is still readable when the note is shared again). A note takes at most 20 promotion requests; their IDs are `<note>.p<n>`, found directly (no scan of the installation), and `promotions` without a note ID scans only the first 4,000 requests. Owner deletion also blanks the `reason` and `path` of the note's promotion requests (marked `scrubbed`; the review ID and digest stay). `list` refuses a malformed `afterId`.

## V01 notes: health and progress stall detection (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/health/` (`assess.ts`: the pure `assessStall`; `stall.ts`: `StallService`), 21 tests per backend in `stall.test.ts` (84 runs over the four backends; the pure test runs in each loop). New record kinds `stall-evidence` (one per task, the task ID), `stall-concern` (ID `stl-<task>-<generation>-<kind>`, so a task's concerns are one contiguous range). No new action names, no new runtime dependency.

- **What it records (bounded evidence).** The owner, a host with `attempt.work` in the task's project, or the owning agent itself reports one event at a time under the generation it believes current: `request_started` / `request_finished` (the controller measures the duration with its own clock), `step` (label and an optional `actionKey`, the reporter's normal form of "the same action"), `error` (class and optional key), `job` (`running`, `waiting`, `idle`, `finished`, `failed`), `spend` (cumulative minutes and/or cost, never decreasing), `evidence` (a named piece of acceptance evidence). Reporters send no times (refused as unknown fields). The record keeps the last step, last error, request, job, spend, the latest 30 evidence names plus a total, and a window of the last 30 steps and errors. Credential-shaped text is refused. A report under a generation that is not current is `fenced`; a new generation starts its evidence again; another agent, a host without the project's grant, or a read-only holder is refused like a missing task. A repeat of a command ID is recorded once; concurrent reports retry on the record's revision.
- **How a long job differs from a loop and a dead worker.** `assessStall` (no clock of its own; `now` is passed in) gives `healthy`, `long_running` (a request is past its limit but the worker has reported since it began), `waiting`, `suspected` (with concerns) or `not_running`. Concerns: `loop` (the same `actionKey` N times since the latest acceptance evidence), `repeated_errors` (same), `silent` (no *progress* and no counted activity since the attempt began, for the silence limit, unless the job reported `finished`), `stuck_request` (a request open past its limit with no progress since it began), `budget` (reported spend at or over the contract's `maxMinutes` or `maxCost`, with the evidence count against the required evidence). New acceptance evidence answers a loop: counts restart. **Only progress counts as liveness**: a step, an error, new evidence, a finished or newly started request, or a *change* of job status. A repeated `job: running` ping or a spend report is a report but not progress, so a hung worker pinging every minute is `silent` and `stuck_request` at the thresholds (tested). Repeats are counted in a bounded map per key since the latest evidence (at most 60 keys, the least repeated dropped), separate from the 30-entry display window, so a multi-action cycle is detected at hard and expert thresholds (tested).
- **Thresholds by difficulty (provisional, configurable).** From the task contract's difficulty (none stated is `medium`): trivial request 2 min / silence 5 min / 4 repeated actions / 3 repeated errors; easy 5/10/5/4; medium 10/20/6/5; hard 20/40/8/6; expert 45/90/10/8. Override per level with `StallOptions.thresholds` (validated). These are guesses to be measured, like C07's shares.
- **Stale timestamps never permit a takeover.** Every assessment carries `takeover: { permitted: false, basis, note }`. `permitted` is always false (nothing here reassigns, releases or fences; a scan changes only concern records, tested against the assignment and task revision). `basis` is `may_be_proposed` only when the worker is silent *and* the host that has been reporting has a **revoked identity** (a fact, not a timestamp). A stale host with a silent worker is `insufficient`: both going quiet is what a network partition looks like. Silence alone, a never-announced host, a fresh host or a worker with no host is `insufficient` too. (A host that heartbeats fresh while reporting the worker gone would also qualify, but there is no such report yet.) Reassigning stays the owner's explicit command (S02).
- **Whose activity counts.** H01's observed activity counts as liveness only under the current generation and only if `observedBy` is the task's owner or the same identity that has been reporting evidence (the H01 note asked for exactly this comparison); an unrelated host's progress report does not keep a task looking alive.
- **Waits (S08).** A task with an open wait is `waiting`, never suspected; a suspected concern clears when a wait opens. Silence of a task that is not in progress with an active attempt is not assessed.
- **Routing (H11).** Each concern records `route`: the owning agent's supervisor (the agent record's `supervisorId`, active), otherwise the installation owner, with the reason (no agent record because it was enrolled by hand, no supervisor, supervisor retired, owner is not an agent). It is recomputed on each scan, so moving the agent moves the concern. The supervisor reads concerns routed to it with no grants; the owning agent reads its own; holders of `assignment.read` or `assignment.manage` in the project read; anyone else gets the answer a missing concern gets.
- **Suspected apart from confirmed.** Concern states: `suspected`, `confirmed`, `dismissed`, `cleared`. `list` defaults to `suspected`, `overview` returns the suspected and confirmed lists separately. `decide` (the owner, the routed supervisor or an `assignment.manage` holder; never the task's own owner, even with that grant) confirms or dismisses with a reason and the revision read; the revision and state are checked inside the command so a repeat returns the recorded answer. Confirming changes nothing about the task or assignment: it is a recorded judgement that a person acts on.
