A dismissal silences that concern for `dismissForMs` (6 h; accepted by the lead as a guess to be measured, like the thresholds) and a still-bad signal then reopens it (`reopened` counts). A scan clears a suspected concern whose signals recovered, whose task stopped running or whose generation ended, reopens a cleared one that comes back, and never touches a confirmed one while its task is running under the same generation. A confirmed concern is **archived** (state `cleared`, reason `archived: ...`, the decision kept) when its task is done or cancelled or its generation has changed.
- **`scan`.** Owner or a service holding `recovery.read` on `*`; one page of assignments (`limit` up to 1000, `afterId`), returns counts and `next`. Stored concern details do not vary with time (they state thresholds, not minutes or counts), and only a change of route rewrites a suspected concern, so a revision a supervisor listed stays valid across scans (tested). Each change is its own command keyed by concern and revision, so a pass repeated after a crash is harmless; a lost race counts as `contended` and the next pass redoes it. Nothing is written when nothing changed.
- **Who may report.** Any host holding `attempt.work` in the project can report on any task there, not only tasks it works on, until H03 links workers to hosts (the same as `HostService.reportProgress`); the record names the reporter truthfully and the owning agent reports only for itself. A late report of an earlier generation is refused inside the command as well as before it, so it cannot overwrite a newer generation's evidence. Host freshness uses the shared `hostFreshness` and `DEFAULT_HOST_STALE_AFTER_MS` exported from `hosts/hosts.ts`. `scan` needs a service holding `recovery.read` exactly (not through `*`).
- **Limits.** There is no timer: nothing calls `scan` yet (the reconciler pass or a scheduler must, and must pass the injected clock); concerns are not yet delivered anywhere (no notification, U11, or chat message), they are records the supervisor lists; nothing yet sends the reports (the worker and dispatcher cards H12/C07b must report request starts, steps with action keys, job status and evidence); the controller cannot tell whether an `actionKey` really names equivalent actions, so a worker that varies it defeats loop detection (and a worker that never reports is caught only by silence); host freshness is read from the host record (`hostStaleAfterMs`, default the shared constant); a worker not linked to a host (H03) leaves `host` absent; `budget` uses the worker's reported spend, not R02's cumulative budget records; the scan reads each assignment's concerns with a range read, which is fine for hundreds of tasks per page, not for very large installations; `job: failed` is shown in signals but is not a concern (an attempt that ends failed is S02's confirmed failure).

## R05 notes: cost and resource attribution and delegated budgets (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/budgets/budgets.ts` (`BudgetService`), 64 tests (16 per backend, four backends). New actions `budget.manage` and `budget.read`; new id prefixes `bgr` (reservation), `bgq` (request), `bgl` (ledger entry). Not yet exposed through the state API (see limits).

- **Two figures, never converted.** Provider spend is `costMicros` (millionths of one currency per tree, chosen when the owner opens the root budget) and local compute is `computeMs` (occupied worker-slot milliseconds, the R05/production-and-capacity basis: the dispatcher reports time an admitted attempt held its slot, and excludes waits after release and suspended time). Every limit, hold and total is kept for both separately; a request for either or both is valid, and running out of one does not touch the other (tested).
- **One record per task tree, so reservation is atomic.** The root's allowance and every delegated sub-allowance live in one `budget-tree` record (at most 200 tasks, 8 levels, 500 open reservations). Each reserve, delegation, settlement or decision is one guarded write of that record; a loser of a race reads again and finds the allowance gone (80 tries, short random pause). Tested: ten concurrent reservations of 15 against 100 give exactly six; two children asking at once for 60 of 100 give one; a delegation racing a reservation never exceeds the node.
- **Delegation moves, it does not copy.** `delegate` (owner, or the holder of the parent node with `budget.manage`) takes the child's allowance out of the parent's available figure in the same step. A node's available figure is its limit less what it delegated, holds (in flight and uncertain) and has spent; a task only ever reserves from its own node. `reclaim` hands a finished child's unused allowance back. Children must be tasks of the tree's project and in no other tree.
- **Reserve, then reconcile.** The dispatcher (owner, host or service) `reserve`s an estimate before dispatch (refused with `invalid_command`, field `costMicros`, message starting `budget:` when it does not fit; a task with no budget is `not_found`, and whether such a task may be dispatched anyway is the dispatcher's policy). `settle` replaces the estimate with actual use: a lower actual gives the difference back; a higher one is recorded as **overrun** only for the part beyond the free allowance: overrun is the increase in how far the node's commitments (spent, handed on, held) exceed its limit across the step, so it is counted once however many other holds are open (tested: 60 and 40 held, 70 then 40 settled gives overrun 10; a charge of 10 while 100 is held, then settling at 100, gives 10). It is already spent and blocks further reservations until covered. A child's overrun is passed up: each ancestor's handed-on figure grows by the excess, so a parent's available figure shows what its children really consumed, and past the parent's own free allowance it becomes the parent's overrun too. `release` returns a reservation that was never dispatched. Each settlement writes one usage-ledger entry named after the reservation; repeating the command is a replay, a different settle command on a settled reservation is `invalid_transition`.
- **Uncertain and outstanding charges are shown.** `markUncertain` moves a reservation to `uncertain` (the host lost the call): it stays held at its full amount and cannot be released, only settled with what really happened. `report` lists in-flight, uncertain and `outstanding` (their sum) per task and the subtree's totals, with every reservation and the reason an uncertain one is uncertain.
- **Attribution.** Ledger entries name task, agent, project, activity (`model-call`, `background-job`, `meeting`, `supervision`, `review`) and category: `work` (a task) or `shared-overhead`. `charge` takes use that was not reserved and requires exactly one of `taskId` (the work it served; it then counts against that task's budget, as overrun if it exceeds what was free) or `sharedOverheadRule` (written text, kept with every entry and listed in `usage().overheadRules`); neither or both is refused, so there is no silent default to a P or PC task. `reserve` takes an `activity` (default `model-call`), kept on the reservation and written to the ledger entry. `usage` totals by category, activity, agent, task and project (money and compute side by side; it scans at most 5000 entries in storage order, which is by ID hash, so when `truncated` is true the figures are a partial sample, not the oldest or newest entries) and needs `budget.read` for the project, or for every project when no project is named (shared overhead belongs to none).
- **Request more budget.** `requestMore` (by someone with `attempt.work` or `budget.manage` on the task) goes to the holder of the parent node, or to the owner when it is a root or the parent has no holder. The owner's `decide` adds **new money** to the task and to every ancestor above it (so no available figure above falls); the addressed holder, with `budget.manage`, can approve only by moving allowance from its own available figure (refused, "ask for more yourself", if it has too little). Grants may be smaller than asked; a denial changes nothing; a decided request is final; at most five open per task; reasons and notes are refused if they look like credentials. A request is readable by its requester, its addressee and the owner. `pending(by)` lists the open requests addressed to the caller (the owner: all owner-addressed ones) without knowing their tasks; it scans at most 5000 request records in hash order and says `truncated`. **No outbox or notification entry is written here** (the budget service has no dependency on U11): U11 or the dispatcher must call `pending` to tell the owner or the holder. The owner may also `raise` directly.
- **Provider ceiling enforceability, stated.** `BUDGET_ENFORCEMENT` is returned in every report: the controller gate is exact for what is dispatched through it; a reservation is an estimate and a provider may charge more (recorded as overrun, a call already sent cannot be recalled); a hard ceiling at the provider (account cap, key limit, prepaid balance) is not set, checked or enforced here, so without one the bound on total spend is the reservations in flight plus any overrun; unreserved work (background, meetings, supervision) is recorded as reported and cannot be refused beforehand.
- **Limits and what later cards must know.** (1) R02's per-task cap (`FallbackService.setBudget` and its `msp-` spend entries) is separate and unchanged; the dispatcher (H12) is expected to `reserve` first, run `FallbackService.begin`/`succeeded`/`failed`, then `settle` with the reported usage, and to report slot time as `computeMs`. Nothing connects the two automatically. (2) Nothing enforces the compute figure on a running process; it is accounting and a gate on new dispatch. (3) Lead decisions to put to the owner: the allowance stays an explicit act, but a project-level allowance opened by the owner with a supervisor holder is the intended default, so only new money and raises reach the owner. The S10 contract `maxCost`, R02's per-task cap and R05 allowances are **three caps** that H12 must reconcile (H12 reserves, calls the fallback service, then settles); none is read by the others. Owner approval creating new money up the path is accepted. Requests do not expire yet, and limits can be lowered only by `reclaim` for now. (4) Not in the state API yet (no `operations.ts` entries), and no owner notification when a request is raised (U11 can read `pendingRequests`). (5) A request is not expired and the owner cannot lower a limit, only `reclaim` unused allowance from a child. (6) The tree record is one serialised write point (fine for dozens of concurrent calls per tree, the thing to split if a single tree grows large); the ledger scan in `usage` is bounded, so very old histories need a periodic fold. (7) Measurement of meeting and supervision effort and the overhead rule itself remain open per production-and-capacity.md; this card only forces them to be named. (8) PC budgets, floors and ceilings (card text of production-and-capacity) are not here.

## T05 notes: the durable conversation through MCP (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/mcp/conversation-adapter.ts` (`McpConversationAdapter`), 3 tests per backend (12 in all, mutation-checked).

- **Same service, no new state.** The adapter takes the `ConversationService` and `PrincipalService`; each `tools/call` authenticates the bearer credential afresh (a bad, disabled or revoked one answers `unauthenticated` on that call; `host` and `service` principals are refused) and calls the service as that principal. Records, sender, scoping, grants, limits, redaction, the client rules (`claimedSender` grants nothing, no notices) and idempotency are U02's; nothing is decided here.
- **Tools.** `conversation_start`, `_send`, `_acknowledge`, `_handled`, `_close` (commands, each needs a `commandId` the client keeps across a reconnect: a repeat is the same message with `replayed: true`, a changed repeat is `command_conflict`) and `conversation_read`, `_thread`, `_threads`, `_unread` (queries, no `commandId`). Both directions work: an agent or an outside client can start a thread and either can answer.
- **Cursors.** `conversation_read` returns the service's items plus `cursor` (the last sequence number returned, else the `after` asked for, else the reader's confirmed position). Reading changes nothing; a client resumes with `after: <cursor>` or simply reads from its confirmed position, so a dropped connection loses and repeats nothing.
- **Protocol session is not the owner.** The adapter keeps no session: `initialize` issues no session id, tools work without it, and no thread, cursor, delivery or task is attached to a handshake or connection. Ownership is the authenticated principal. A thread view has no task or session field, and talking creates no task.
- **Protocol upgrade.** `initialize` accepts only `MCP_SUPPORT.revisions` (otherwise an error listing them, as in T04). Because the records are the service's, reconnecting with a new adapter instance and the other revision returns identical reads and thread lists, the same cursor, replays an old `commandId` as the same message, and leaves the audit records written earlier unchanged (tested on `listAudit`). "Audit history" here is the store's audit trail plus the messages and deliveries naming the authenticated sender; the adapter writes none of its own.
- **Unreadable reads like missing.** A thread another principal or project may not see answers `not_found` for read and thread view and `forbidden` for send and acknowledge, in each case with the same text as a thread that does not exist (tested); listings show only the caller's threads. Unknown arguments (`sender`, `actor`) are refused by the service's `onlyKnown` (and for queries by the adapter), so the sender cannot be set from the content. Error messages and `field` are passed through `redactText` (a first draft echoed an unknown argument name unredacted in `field`; a test now covers it); internal faults answer generically.
- **Does not do.** No transport (stdio, streamable HTTP framing, SSE) and so no session header; no `resources`, `prompts` or `listChanged`/push notifications (readers poll `conversation_read`; the U02b HTTPS long-poll remains the push-like path); no `conversation_thread` resource URIs; batches are refused. The tools are not mounted in `startController` and the package index does not export the MCP area (as with T04).
- **Later cards must know.** Task-scoped principals pass straight through to the service, which applies their `taskScope`; the adapter adds nothing there. Whoever adds the transport must pass the bearer credential to `handle` per request and must not cache the principal.
- **Credentials (lead decisions).** Agents converse under their own credential; task credentials (`tcr-`) are not accepted by this adapter (same limit as U02b). The owner's credential is accepted and may use this door. `host` and `service` credentials are refused with `unauthenticated` (tested). A later transport card must not add an `Mcp-Session-Id` that carries state.
- **Cursor edge case.** The service scans at most about 5000 messages after the cursor; if more than that are hidden from the reader, a read returns no items and `cursor` equals `after`. `lastSeq` in the result lets a client detect that it is behind.

## C07b notes: context budgets enforced at dispatch, bounded compaction (2026-10-22)

Controller: `packages/controller/src/context/dispatch.ts` (pure functions; `dispatch.test.ts`, 10 tests) and `enforcement.ts` (`ContextEnforcementService`; `enforcement.test.ts`, 18 tests on each of the four backends). Pi adapter: `packages/pi-adapter/src/context-policy.ts` (`PiAdapterOptions.contextPolicy`; `context-policy.test.ts`, 5 tests). Small edits elsewhere: a new blocker type `context_overflow` (S07's `BlockerType`) and the id prefixes `cmp`, `adm`, `hof`, `spl`, `ctx`. The dispatcher (H12) does not exist, so this is a service with a stated interface that it will call; nothing here starts an attempt.

- **Who may call.** `admit`, `confirmUsage`, `changeRoute`, `registerCompressor`, `compact`, `reportOverflow`, `resetOverflow`, `limitTool` and `ensureHandoff` are the dispatcher's: owner, host or service principals only (the same gate as R01's model calls), never an agent whatever its grants. `recordHandoff` (the attempt's own hand-off), `readSpill`, `getCompaction`, `readSummary`, `latestCompaction`, `keptDecisions`, `latestHandoff` and `handoffsOf` need `attempt.work` on the task, and read like nothing to anyone else.

- **Admission (`admit`, `confirmUsage`, `changeRoute`).** The first call is measured as Pi's system prompt plus every tool's schema (characters, as the caller measures them) plus what the startup builder adds, converted at the budget's characters per token; a size the provider reported for the same prompt (a token-count probe or an earlier call of the same prefix) wins when larger. Two limits: the first call at most the startup share, and first call plus likely tool output (default one result at the per-tool limit) plus the reply reserve at most the working share. A refusal is a durable record keyed by task, attempt and round and opens one visible `context_overflow` blocker on the task; a repeat finds the same record. While such a blocker is open nothing new is admitted on the task. After a real call `confirmUsage` stores the reported input size, the characters per token it implies (feed it back as the `charsPerToken` override) whether the call was in fact over the startup limit, and (per task and route, a route defaulting to one per window) the ratio of reported to estimated size; later `admit` calls on that route scale their estimate by that ratio (never below 1), so the same prompt asked again after an over-limit confirmation is refused. A size the provider reported for the very call is used as given. `changeRoute` builds the startup view again for a smaller-window route with that route's own fixed part (C07's builder), returns the cut text for archiving, or blocks with the numbers and a blocker; nothing is sent in that case.
- **One compressor per context (`registerCompressor`).** One record per task; a different second compressor is refused (`invalid_command`), the same one again is a no-op, and `compact` refuses any compressor but the registered one. Two registering at once: one wins.
- **Bounded compaction (`compact`, `boundedSummarise`).** A chain of summarisers, each under its own hard timeout (default 120 s, 300 s in all), then a deterministic extractive fallback (start and end of the text plus the pinned decisions, credentials removed; switch off with `extractive: false`). A summary must be non-empty, at most the short summary limit (2,000 characters) and at most half the size of the text it replaces; anything else counts as not produced (the Hermes message "without reducing this conversation" is exactly this case). If nothing is produced the compaction fails as `context_overflow`, is recorded as failed (a repeat answers from the record) and counts toward the overflow blocker. A compaction that loses the race for the chain is closed as `superseded` (failed, not an overflow) instead of staying started. `compact` takes the real `attemptId`, and a failed compaction counts under it, so one attempt that fails to compact and then overflows counts once (the later report is the same attempt). The summarisers get an `AbortSignal`, which `compact` also takes from the caller (the adapter hook): when the caller gives up, the summarisers are told to stop and nothing is recorded as done or published (the record stays `started` and the key resumes later); a summary that arrives after the abort is dropped. Defaults: 45 s per route, 100 s for the chain, against the adapter's 150 s ceiling, so the extractive fallback is reached before the adapter declines. The extractive summary is marked as such in its text and its record (`route: controller-extractive`, `degraded`) so the owner can see it, is on by default, and does not count toward the overflow count because it is a degraded result, not an overflow. A summariser that ignores its abort signal is simply no longer waited for. Timers are real timers (not the services' injected clock), so tests use short real timeouts.
- **Restart safety.** One record per (task, key): `started`, then `done` with the summary text (one atomic write that also moves the task's chain head), then the retrieval summary is published and its ID recorded. A repeat after a crash at any point resumes from the record: a finished compaction is answered without calling a summariser; if two runs of one key race, the first recorded result stands and the other adopts it (storage reports the second write as a conflict, which `#firstWriterWins` treats as "someone finished"). Tested by killing the process between the record and the publication, and by two concurrent runs.
- **Lineage and owner decisions.** Each compaction names its `parent`, which must be the task's latest one (a line, not a tree; two different compactions at once: one wins and the other is closed as superseded, or refused at the start if the winner had already finished), its generation and the full list of ancestors. `pinned` source links (owner decisions, desk records or history entries, at most 12) are carried through every later compaction and written into every summary as a short line each (first 80 characters), so they survive repeated compactions and a change of model; `keptDecisions` reads them again with the reader's rights (`unavailable` when gone or not readable).
- **Readable only with every source (A10).** The compressed view is a retrieval summary (C02): it keeps the source project and origins with digests, and `readSummary` withholds the text from anyone who cannot read every source; `getCompaction` never returns the stored text. Unreadable reads like missing.
- **Overflow (`reportOverflow`, `resetOverflow`).** Counted once per attempt, only for messages `classifyFailure` calls context overflow (a rate-limit lookalike is not counted). The first (below `maxOverflows`, default 2) says restart fresh from the continuation record; at the limit a `context_overflow` blocker opens and the answer is `blocked`. The owner resolving the blocker, or a good attempt, calls `resetOverflow`.
- **Tool output (`limitTool`, `readSpill`, `piOutputLimits`).** One gate for Pi's tools and MCP tools: C07's `limitToolOutput` at the window's limit, the remainder stored (credentials removed from the whole remainder first, then cut to 256,000 characters, the rest reported as lost) under a spill ID and read back in pages by anyone who may work on the task. In the adapter, `contextPolicy.toolOutputBytes/Lines` set Pi's own `outputLimits` on every adapter tool (`outputRetain: "tail"` for command output); `piOutputLimits(budget)` gives the numbers.
- **Hand-off (`recordHandoff`, `ensureHandoff`, `latestHandoff`).** When an attempt ends, `ensureHandoff` returns the attempt's own hand-off if there is one, else writes one with C07's `handoffFromFacts` from facts the dispatcher supplies (a callback: the ledger, artifacts, checkpoint, last state), once per attempt, and attaches it to the task. The attempt's own hand-off and the controller's are separate records: an own one arriving after the controller's is kept beside it and preferred as the task's latest (`handoffsOf` shows both). Tested with a real effect ledger: a confirmed merge, no hand-off from the attempt, a controller hand-off listing it with credentials removed.
- **Pi adapter.** `contextPolicy` sets the stream timeout and retries explicitly (merged over `settings.stream`), tool limits, and a `beforeCompact` callback. The callback gets the plain text of what is being replaced and a key that is the same after a restart; `request.signal` aborts when the adapter stops waiting; its first answer is stored as a Pi memo, so a restart asks again only if nothing was stored and never stores two. The adapter has its own ceiling (default 150 s) on the callback. Found by test: when a Pi hook throws, Pi ignores the hook and runs its own summariser (the model that is out of room, with whatever timeout the stream has), so the adapter turns a timeout or throw into a remembered decline and reports it through `onHookFailure` (after one timeout the decline is remembered for that compaction: accepted); the conversation then overflows on its next request and the attempt ends as `context_overflow`.
- **Tool limits and the resume guard.** MCP tools get the window-based limit only through `limitTool`, which the MCP layer must call; `frameResult` in the MCP package still uses the fixed `MCP_LIMITS.text`. C07's `resumeDecision` has no dispatch hook yet: the dispatcher must call it before resuming (H12).
- **Lead decisions recorded.** Defaults of 2 overflows and 12 pinned decisions are accepted. One open `context_overflow` blocker per task, reusing the first reason's text for a second, is accepted.
- **Limits stated.** Nothing is wired to a dispatcher, a model or a real provider; the shape of the dispatcher's calls is the tested interface. Sizes are character estimates until `reportedInputTokens`/`confirmUsage` give real ones. Compaction material, sources and facts come from the caller (the controller cannot yet read Pi's transcript or list a task's effects; there is no effect-by-task index). One blocker per task of type `context_overflow` is open at a time, so a second reason reuses the first one's text. The spill store is in the controller's records (bounded), not in artifacts. The extractive fallback is crude on purpose. Pi's own thresholds (reserve, keep-recent, background) are not retuned here; Pi still compacts on them and now asks the hook. Model fallback itself is R02's: this card only rebuilds the view and blocks when it cannot.

## R03 notes: difficulty profiles and escalation (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/routing/difficulty.ts` (`DifficultyService`), tests in `difficulty.test.ts` (22 tests on each of the four backends). New id prefixes `rdc` (routing decision) and `esc` (escalation request). One small addition to S10: `ContractService.difficultyChanges` (below).

- **Difficulty is not priority.** Difficulty stays the task's own contract field (S10); this service never reads priority, and `setDifficulty` and escalation change nothing but the difficulty (each is a normal stored contract version). A test changes priority and shows the decision's profile, route and request are identical, and changes difficulty and shows priority is unchanged.
- **Profiles and mapping.** The owner (or a holder of `routing.write`) replaces one record, `setProfiles` (revisioned, `expectedRevision` required, all-or-nothing), holding named execution profiles and the difficulty-to-profile mapping. A profile has: `harness`, `provider` + `model` (given together, must be a route in the routing table), `routingProfile` (an R01 profile whose routes follow), `reasoning` (`effort` none/low/medium/high, `maxThinkingTokens`), `contextTokens`, `needs` (capabilities), `tools` (action-name ceiling) and `spendLimitMicros`. A profile must name a route or a routing profile; **nothing has a built-in value**: with no record, or a difficulty with no mapping, or a task with no difficulty and no `unrated` mapping, the dispatch is a `no-choice` decision that says so. Validation checks against the routing table of that moment (`setProfiles` is refused while there is no table).
- **Dispatch.** `resolve` (dispatcher only: owner, host or service) reads the task's difficulty, the mapping and the routing table as they are now, folds the profile's context and capability needs into the request, and applies the R01 rules (`selectRoute`) to the profile's route list (the explicit provider/model first, then the routing profile's routes). A `spendLimitMicros` of 0 means no paid route. The decision (difficulty, profile name and the profile as it was, both revisions used, route or the reasons, every candidate's reasons, the request) is stored under the task and returned; repeating the command ID returns the recorded decision. Revision checks on the contract, profiles and routing records make the decision describe the state at its commit. A later change of mapping, profiles or table therefore touches only the next dispatch; stored decisions keep what they used (tested).
- **Locality, provider and model are never implied by a tier.** The test maps trivial to a cloud model and expert to a local one; data that may not leave the installation, an unauthorized paid route, a missing capability and an excluded route are all handled by the unchanged R01 rules, with the reasons in the decision.
- **Who sets difficulty.** `delegate` hands a task to a child agent: the delegator becomes the **parent**, and sets a ceiling and two budgets. Delegating needs `task.difficulty.write` on the task (`task.contract.write` alone is refused: otherwise anyone who can edit a contract could make themselves parent, set difficulty and raise the ceiling with nobody approving). The owner can replace the parent with `reassignParent` (owner only, recorded in the audit and `setBy`; the new parent must be an active agent other than the child), for example when the parent agent is retired. The parent sets difficulty through `setDifficulty` with no grant (it still needs to read the task); anyone else goes through the S10 path and needs `task.difficulty.write` (a grant narrowed to the task works). The child, an outsider, and a principal with only other grants (for example `task.contract.write`) are refused. A task nobody delegated has no parent: the owner and grant holders only. The owner changing a delegation does not become its parent.
- **Escalation.** The child (only the child) calls `escalate` with `kind` `blocked` (names an **open blocker whose subject is the task**) or `failing` (the assignment has had at least 2 attempts admitted; a proxy, see limits) and a reason (refused if credential-shaped). "Failing" counts attempts admitted since the last request that changed the difficulty (all of them before the first), and a blocker is evidence for one request only. The target is the next level up unless `to` names a higher one (at the top, nothing to escalate to). At or under the **ceiling** the difficulty is set in the same step (`applied`). Above it the request is `pending` for the parent, who (or the owner, or a holder of `task.difficulty.write`) `decide`s: approving sets the difficulty and raises the ceiling to the target; denying records the reason and changes nothing else. One request waits at a time. All of it is one command per step, so the request record, the delegation counter and the contract change commit together.
- **Loop limits.** `retryBudget` (default 2) counts earlier requests for the same target (a denied one asked again counts); `totalBudget` (default 5) counts every request. The request that would pass either is stored as `stopped` with the reason (and no route), once; later requests are refused (`invalid_transition`) and write nothing, so a loop writes at most budget + 1 records per round. The parent (or owner) can `decide` a stopped request like a waiting one (approving applies it and raises the ceiling), and that decision, or a redelegation, begins a new round: the budgets count only the current round's requests. The parent can also set difficulty directly. The stored requests are always kept.
- **Persisted and queryable.** `decisions` (paged), `decision`, `escalations`, `delegation`, `profiles`. Visible to whoever can read the task, and to a task's parent and child; anyone else gets the answer a missing task would give (empty list or undefined).
- **Concurrency.** Every escalation and decision touches the delegation record at the revision it read, and guards the contract record, so two at once cannot both pass the checks. The contract version read at the start is passed on when the difficulty is applied (escalation and approval), so a parent's own setting that lands in between makes the step fail with `stale_revision` instead of being overwritten by a lower level (tested with a storage hook). (The loser gets `stale_revision`; two sequential ones form one chain, tested for no two steps from the same level). The price is that one task's escalations are serialized.
- **Added to S10.** `ContractService.difficultyChanges(actor, taskId, difficulty|null, expectedVersion?)` returns the storage changes for a new contract version that sets difficulty, so another command can commit them with its own records; it checks no authority (the caller has decided). It is covered through the R03 tests only.

Not done and limits:
- Not in the state API or `api-v1.md` yet (the service is complete; operations are a later wiring step).
- `FallbackService.begin` (R02) still takes a request with a routing profile name. A decision whose profile names a `routingProfile` can be passed on with that profile and the chosen route as `prefer`; a profile that names only provider/model has no routing profile, so the dispatcher should use the decision's `route` directly. The dispatcher loop (H12) is where `resolve` and `begin` meet.
- `spendLimitMicros`, `tools`, `harness` and `reasoning` are recorded in the decision's profile snapshot; only a limit of 0 acts here (no paid route). Holding the task to a limit is R02/R05's budget, which the owner sets separately; enforcing `tools` is the tool path (T06, H12); passing `harness` and `reasoning` to the worker is the adapter's.
- "Failing" is judged by attempts admitted (2 or more), not by their outcomes (the assignment record counts all admitted attempts); a finer test would read attempt outcomes. A child that is failing but whose parent is a person is handled the same way: the request waits for the parent or owner.
- Lead decisions recorded: "parent" means whoever delegated the task with difficulty authority, not the H11 supervisor (no check against H11 records); a parent has no authority beyond difficulty and delegation of this task. The per-task ceiling is chosen by the parent and accepted as given; delegation chains are not limited by the parent's own ceiling for now. A single `unrated` mapping key is how a task with no difficulty is treated.
- If the routing table is later replaced so that a profile's route or routing profile is gone, the profile stays and decisions say `not a configured route` rather than refusing the table change.

## A03b notes: the Windows Sandbox launcher (2026-10-22)

**Status: code and simulated tests done; not qualified on Windows.** Nothing here has run against a real Windows Sandbox. The card stays `planned` until the real run (below) is green and reviewed.

**What it is.** A `windows-sandbox` backend behind the A03 `SandboxBackend` interface (`packages/worker/src/sandbox/windows.ts`, with `windows-plan.ts` for the pure rules, `windows-job.ts` for the job, `windows-runner.ts` for the process the launcher starts, `windows-host.ts` for the interface to the machine and `windows-real.ts` for the real implementation). Every process and file effect (wsb, icacls, PowerShell, the registry reads, the file system) goes through `WindowsHost`; the unit tests use an in-memory Windows host. The launcher starts one supervisor runner (`node windows-runner.js <job file>`) per job; the runner takes a lock (only one Windows Sandbox can exist at a time; a second job exits 75 "busy" and never disturbs the first), re-checks the client and the session, grants the narrow ACLs, runs `wsb start --config <xml>` from the package folder, re-checks the session, runs the command with `wsb exec` as guest SYSTEM, then stops the sandbox, takes the ACLs back and removes the job.

**Rules implemented (each has simulated tests).**
- *Fail closed.* No capacity unless: not-Windows is refused; the Microsoft package `MicrosoftWindows.WindowsSandbox` with publisher id `8wekyb3d8bbwe`, version at least 0.8.107.0 (the version researched), installed under `%ProgramFiles%\WindowsApps`, with `wsb.exe` and `WindowsSandboxServer.exe` present and validly signed by Microsoft; the client is always started with its package folder as working directory (the research showed it fails otherwise); the console session belongs to the configured owner; and a real probe sandbox shows the guest cannot write a read-only input, cannot write inside `.git`, cannot reach the network, and can write its checkout (the host then checks the files). All of this is repeated at the start of every job, because health may be old.
- *Mode in the audit.* `LaunchRecord.backendDetails` (new, from the backend's `auditDetails`) carries `windowsMode`, `sessionDependent: "true"`, the client version, the share account, the supervisor-ACL policy and a one-line VM decision; `Capacity.notes` (new, from `BackendHealth.notes`) carries the same facts and is reported even when unhealthy.
- *Supervisor code outside guest-writable staging.* The runner, the control directory (job files, lock) and the staging directory may not overlap (refused at construction, at plan time and again at start); no shared folder may overlap the control directory or the runner's folder; health refuses when the control directory or the runner's folder is writable by the share account or a broad group (ACL read with icacls). Only the job's generated `launch.cmd` is shared with the guest, read-only. Nothing from a shared folder is ever run by the supervisor.
- *Narrow per-job ACLs.* One named account (the one the Sandbox service uses; broad groups and system accounts are refused): Modify on checkout, home and scratch, Read on inputs, a deny of writing inside `.git`. Before any change the runner saves each folder's whole access list (`icacls /save` into the job directory), records it atomically in `acl.json`, and only then adds the entry; the only way back is `icacls /restore` of the saved list, so an entry the account already had (explicit, inherited, deny, or a longer account name ending the same way) is never mistaken for ours. If any restore fails, or `acl.json` cannot be read, the job directory with the saved lists and the lock are kept, the failure is returned (`release` throws, the launcher puts it in `RunResult.releaseProblem`, the runner exits non-zero) and health stays unhealthy until the next health check restores them.
- *Escape surface.* No network, no GPU, audio, video, printer or clipboard; only the listed folders; `.git` is shared again read-only on top of the checkout; the launch script sits in a read-only share; host paths are validated (local, normalised, no UNC, no `..`, no 8.3 names, not Windows, Program Files, ProgramData or Users themselves, no links or junctions); command arguments with quotes, percent signs or line breaks are refused instead of escaped.
- *Session dependence and the opt-in mode.* Default: the owner must be signed in at the console. Auto-login and lock is a separate mode that exists only when the configuration holds the owner's authority and the exact acknowledgement sentence; then admission also needs the console confirmed locked (WTS flags, checked at admission and again before the command), auto-login set up locally for the owner with no password in the clear in the registry (booleans only; the value is never read into the program's output), and the lock task present, enabled and running the configured helper, which must be writable by administrators only. Nothing in this code can enable auto-login, create an account, or store or print a credential (the host interface has no such operation, and a test checks that).
- *VM fallback.* Recorded as a documented limit, not built (`VM_FALLBACK_DECISION`): Windows Sandbox meets the lifecycle in its session-dependent forms, and a separate VM would need its own licence and deployment review; the owner declined evaluation media. With nobody signed in and no opt-in, capacity is unavailable and the diagnostic names the 0x8000401A finding.

**Small changes to A03 code** (test-covered): `SandboxBackend` gained optional `auditDetails()` and `release(plan)`, `BackendHealth` gained `notes`, `LaunchRecord` gained `backendDetails`, `Capacity` gained `notes`; the launcher calls `release` after every run (also after a kill or a failed start). Also fixed a latent Windows-only bug: the launcher's own-files protection stripped only a trailing `/`, so on Windows the supervisor root kept its trailing `\` and protected nothing; it now strips either.

**Proven by simulation only (windows.test.ts, 37 tests on any OS).** The order and arguments of every wsb and icacls call, the ACL commands, the configuration XML, refusals of all kinds, busy handling, release after a kill, the mode in the audit, that the probe script asks for each denial, that the probe verdicts are acted on. This proves the rules, not that Windows enforces anything.

**Awaiting the real Windows run** (`windows-real.test.ts`, started by the `sandbox-windows` job of `own-hardware.yml` on the runner labelled `nimblenewt-windows`; it uploads `findings.json`, `output.txt` and `junit.xml`): the escape fixture itself (eight attempts: read-only input, `.git`, host path, host profile, network, the launch script, a junction inside the share); whether Windows honours a nested read-only share for `.git` on top of a writable one; the exact `wsb` command syntax and outputs assumed here (`start --config <xml>` printing an id, `exec --id -c -d -r System`, `stop --id`, `list --raw`), including whether `exec` returns the guest's exit code and output; the PowerShell session, package, signature, task and registry probes (the WTS lock flag at offset 16 is read from a struct layout worked out on paper); that ACLs are restored exactly; that a hard kill ends the sandbox; that the share account assumed (the user running the tests) is the one the Sandbox service actually needs. The auto-login and lock mode has a real test too but it runs only on a machine the owner set up for it and sets `NIMBLENEWT_REAL_WINDOWS_AUTOLOGON=1` and `NIMBLENEWT_WSB_LOCK_HELPER` locally; the workflow never sets them.

**Limits.** One Windows Sandbox at a time, so Windows has capacity one. Resource limits (A08b) are not enforced on Windows (reported "none"). The lock is checked at admission and again before the command starts, not continuously. The strict supervisor-ACL rule cannot pass in a checkout the runner's account can write; the real job therefore sets `report-only` (recorded in the audit) and the test records whether strict would pass. Guest programs run as guest SYSTEM and are named by POSIX-style recipe paths (`/inputs/tools/x.exe`, `/windows/system32/cmd.exe`) that the backend maps to `C:\nn\...`; the agent sees Windows paths in `guestView`. `normalizeRecipe`'s system-directory list is POSIX only; the Windows checks live in the backend. Environment values and arguments are limited to characters safe for a batch file. No Windows-side quarantine run has happened (A03's quarantine is untested on Windows). Hard kill uses `wsb stop`, which is not proven to stop a sandbox with a command in flight until the real run.

**A03b review fixes and decisions (2026-10-22).**
- Each launch has its own job directory named by its run id (`backend.plan` now receives the launcher's run id; both plans of one launch get the same id). A launch refused as busy therefore cannot stop, restore or delete the running job; `releaseJob` touches nothing while another live job holds the lock. A stale lock is moved aside by a rename and judged by what was moved. Health checks run one at a time with a unique probe folder, clean up leftover job directories first, and refuse while a sandbox it did not start is listed. The share-folder link check now compares the real path with the path (a junction higher up is refused). The launcher's own-files root loses a trailing separator of either kind (tested).
- The real tests use the runner's own account as the share account, so a pass cannot show that the narrow grants are what make access work; a later run with a separate account is needed for that claim.
- The uploaded `sandbox-windows` artifact holds only `summary.json` (pass/fail per test, counts, OS build, mode names) because the repository is public. User names, local paths, session, auto-login and lock-task facts and the guest output stay in a local folder on the machine (`NIMBLENEWT_WSB_LOCAL`, with `findings.json`, `output.txt`, `junit.xml`). Failure detail therefore reaches the lead from the machine, not the artifact.
- Report-only supervisor-ACL checking is accepted for the first qualifying run only; G05 must run the strict check before release.
- Refuse-rather-than-queue with capacity one is accepted: a second Windows job exits 75 busy.


## L05 notes: generation interruption and late-result handling (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/generation/` (`GenerationService`, `GenerationRefused`). A *generation* here is one model response streamed for a task (not an assignment generation, S02). L01-L04 are not built yet, so the hold below is this card's own and small; L01's durable pause gate should adopt or replace it (see "For later cards").

- **Records.** `generation-gate` (one per task: context revision, run counter, current run, hold, corrections) and `generation-run` (one per generation: owner, number, the context revision it was bound to, optional S02 fence, state `streaming | finished | held | interrupted`, text, numbered chunks, tool calls, the interruption, late results). Run IDs are derived from the task and the run number (`GenerationService.runIdOf`).
- **Revision-bound partial streams.** `begin` names the context revision it was built on and is refused (`GenerationRefused`, reason `stale`) if that is not current. Chunks are numbered from 1 without gaps (`record`; a repeat of the same command ID is the recorded result). Only the latest run at the current revision is "current"; beginning another run supersedes the previous one, `correct` (raises the revision, caller names the revision it saw, so two corrections cannot both apply) interrupts the running one.
- **Late results.** Anything recorded for an interrupted run (text chunk, tool-call chunk, completion) is **not applied**: it is stored in the run's `late` list (kind, seq, size, `sha256` digest, and for text a 200-character redacted preview), counted in `lateCount` (the list holds 50, the count is exact) and `record`/`complete` answer `late: true, applied: false`. So a late response cannot override a correction, and it is visible to a reader of the run. The late content itself is not kept.
- **Partial tool calls never execute.** A call's arguments stream in; it becomes `complete` only when the stream says `done` and the arguments parse to a JSON object. A call left unfinished when the stream ends or is cut, or finished with malformed arguments, is `dropped` for good; `admitEffect` refuses anything not `complete`/`held` (reason `incomplete`) and anything in a run that is still streaming (reason `streaming`).
- **Controls.** `interrupt(mode)`: `cancel` cuts the run now (unadmitted calls withdrawn, partial calls dropped, state `interrupted`); `finish_and_hold` leaves a streaming run to end, then it becomes `held` (a finished run becomes `held` immediately). Both set a hold on the task in the same commit, so from that commit on `begin`, `admitEffect` and the start of effects **admitted and started through this service** (`execute`) are refused (reason `held`). Effects started any other way (for example slice/pipeline.ts via `EffectService.perform`) are NOT gated unless the starter passes `GenerationService.guardFor(taskId)` as `EffectService.run`'s `beforeStart`; H12 and any dispatcher must wire it into every run for a task (tested: it blocks a run on a held task and does not block recovery of an effect that already started). Asking for the same mode again is refused; a cancel may follow a finish-and-hold. `release` lifts the hold and admits nothing: held calls need an explicit `admitEffect` (readmission, as F06c requires), which is still refused if the run has been superseded or the context corrected since.
- **Superseding.** `begin` may cut another principal's running generation (withdrawing its calls and unstarted effects) only for the installation owner, a holder of `assignment.manage`, or a caller presenting a valid fence; otherwise it is `forbidden`. Own generations are superseded freely.
- **Atomic admission.** `admitEffect` checks hold, run (not interrupted, current, bound revision) and call, and writes the S05 effect intent in the same commit, guarded by the gate and run revisions (and the S02 fence if the run has one). A pause, cancel or correction racing an admission is strictly before or after it (tested by repeated concurrent races). The intent is the same record `EffectService.intend` writes (ID derived from the command ID).
- **Effects around an interruption.** Cancel, correction and supersession withdraw admitted effects that have **not started** (ledger `failed`, evidence names the run; each effect is guarded by its revision so a claim racing the cut either wins, and the effect is left alone, or loses). `execute(runId, toolCallId, bridge)` starts an admitted effect through `EffectService.run` and refuses to start one while held or interrupted; the check commits with the claim, so a pause landing between check and claim makes the claim retry and refuse (tested with a seam). An effect that **has started** is never cut: it settles in the ledger (confirmed, failed or uncertain, S05 rules unchanged), and `reconcile` copies the ledger status onto the call, marked `afterInterruption: true` if the run was interrupted or the task held at that moment, so it is shown as late, not as the generation's current result. `reconcile` can be repeated; a person's `resolve` of an uncertain effect shows on the next one.
- **Authorization.** Streaming and admission: the run's owner with `attempt.work` (admission and `execute` also `effect.perform`) on the task, or the installation owner. Controls (`interrupt`, `release`, `correct`): `assignment.manage` on the task. Reads: the run's owner, a holder of `assignment.read` on the task, the installation owner; others read like a missing record. Credential-shaped text (streamed text, call arguments, reasons) is refused; unknown fields are refused.
- **Change outside the package.** `EffectService.run` takes an optional `{ beforeStart }` (domain/effects.ts): called only for an effect that has not started, may refuse by throwing, and the guard changes it returns commit with the claim. Not used by existing callers. Covered by the generation tests (mutation: dropping its changes fails the seam test).
- **Limits.** (1) The service records and fences; it does not call the runtime. The controller must call `ConversationPort.abort()` (F06c) after `interrupt(cancel)` and feed whatever the runtime still delivers to `record`/`complete`, where it lands as late; it must pass the adapter's retained (withdrawn) requests through readmission as before. (2) Each chunk rewrites the run record (text up to 64 KiB, at most 32 calls of 16 KiB arguments, 8 KiB per chunk), so a long stream costs quadratic writes; the bounds keep it small, and a chunking layer should batch deltas. (3) Runs are never deleted and cannot be listed (read by ID; the gate holds the count). (4) `interrupt` and `release` replayed by command ID report the task's current run, not the one at first application. (5) The hold is per task; an agent-level Pause spanning tasks and the observed states (pausing, stopping) are L01. A cancelled effect that already started cannot be recalled. (6) No outbox message or owner notification announces an interruption or a late result yet. (7) The optional decoder resume named in the card is not built; it is a separate qualification and would use `record` with a fresh run bound to the new revision. (8) `begin` does not check the task is `in_progress` or that the caller holds the assignment unless a fence is given; pass the fence.
- **More limits.** `admitEffect` takes `kind` and `target` freely from the admitter and ignores the call's tool name; the tool mapping and its checks belong to T01/A05. A wrong `expectedContextRevision` is refused at once (not retried).
- **Lead decisions recorded.** The per-task hold in `generation-gate` is accepted until L01 adopts or replaces it (L01 should reconcile it with the `manual_hold` blocker). Both interrupt modes set a hold and a `release` by someone with `assignment.manage` is required before a new generation (a pause is explicit). `GenerationRefused` (`invalid_command` plus a `reason`) is accepted for now, not yet a shared convention.
- **For later cards.** L01 should reuse `generation-gate.hold` (or make `begin`/`admitEffect`/`execute` consult its pause gate in the same guarded commit). L04's resume should bump the context revision with `correct`, so a pre-resume generation can never apply. G09 can drive the whole chain through the production service: stream, partial call, cancel, late completion, correction, readmission.

## H13 notes: event source adapters, watchers and event-to-inbox scheduling (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/events/` gains `adapters.ts` (pure adapters), `sources.ts` (`SourceService`) and `handling.ts` (`WatchService`, `verifyAtResume`); 23 tests on each of four backends in `watchers.test.ts` (mutation-checked, second round included: no lease expiry, failed handlings still counting, retry past the cap or without limit, the origin pointer taking `through`, the reconciler admitting event items, no hand-over to a new owner, waking through a hold, causes dropped by each adapter; no dedup record, no cause links, a hold ignored, held loops ignored, no loop record, no per-owner cap, an unbounded poll, backpressure ignored, a repeated reconcile, no carry-over after a dead handling, failures readable by anyone, resume treating anything but `changed` as clean, a baseline that emits, no cause-before-effect ordering, acknowledgement skipped, and redaction removed at both places each fail tests). `events.ts` (H06) is not changed. New id prefixes `evw` (watch), `evh` (handling), `evx` (everything else keyed by a hash) in `domain/ids.ts`; three read-only API operations `events.failures`, `events.handling`, `events.loops` (the generated `api-v1.md` is regenerated; `StateApiDeps.watches` is optional, absent means `unavailable`).

- **Adapters** are plain objects with `poll({state, now, limit})`: timers (due on the injected clock, once per id and due time), task completion (done or cancelled, once per closed state), files and repositories (snapshot differ), service failures (transitions, once per failure) and model availability (transitions; `availabilityFromFailure` maps R02's classification of credit, rate-limit, outage and authentication failures to "unavailable"). They hold no storage and no clock; what they observe comes from injected probes (tests use scripted ones; the real probes are the worker's). Each signal has a dedup identity, a kind, a stream, a record, an optional key, optional text, an optional cause and an optional originating task.
- **Watchers are hints.** The file and repository adapter does not depend on notices arriving: it compares a snapshot with the last saved one, so a dropped notice is found by the next snapshot, a repeat changes nothing, and two changes between polls give one event for the latest version. The first snapshot is a silent baseline. What decides whether inputs changed is `verifyAtResume`, the direct C03 check against the worker's current observations, which never reads the event stream (tested with every watcher event dropped; no observation reads as not clean, never as unchanged).
- **`SourceService.run`** (owner, host or service) polls each source in turn and publishes into H06. A signal already published is recognised from its own record (`evx-cause`) and skipped; the publish command ID derives from source and dedup, so a crash between publishing and recording, or two runs at once, give one event. A run takes at most 200 signals per source (1000 at most), saves the adapter's state only after publishing (a refused or crashed run polls the same state again), a failing source is reported (redacted, cut) without stopping the others, a malformed signal is rejected and listed without blocking the source, and when H06 says a critical subscriber is full the run stops for that source (`slowed`) with nothing lost.
- **Causation and loops.** A signal names an earlier signal (`causedBy`, same source unless it says otherwise; within one poll causes are published first) or the task whose work caused it (`originTask`): while that task is handling an event (an open pointer record per task), the change is linked to the event it was handling. H06 suppresses an event that repeats its own ancestry; H13 then **holds the loop**: a held-loop record for that stream, kind and record, which the dispatcher consults, so later events with the same signature are not scheduled (reported as `skipped`) until the owner releases it; other events carry on. `loops` lists held loops (owner, and readers of a `project:` stream); only the owner releases.
- **Watch and dispatch.** `watch` (owner or service) ties an H06 subscription to a task: the stream must be the task's own or its project's, the subscriber is the dispatching principal (so it is a critical subscriber and its backlog slows producers). `dispatch` makes at most one batch: it records a **handling** (the attempt) together with the watch's `inflight` marker before anything is delivered, puts an inbox item (S04, source `events`, delivery ID derived from the handling) in front of the task's **current** owner, acknowledges the events, then marks the handling `scheduled`; a crash at any point resumes the same handling (tested by failing the acknowledgement once). Events wait, and nothing is lost, when: the stream's scope is on manual hold (`held`), the subscription is cooling down (`cooling`, with `retryAt`), the task has no owner (`unowned`), or the owner already has two open handlings (`busy`). Events never lift any of these. Missing, skipped or expired events make the item say `reconcile: true` with reasons, once per distinct set of reasons (a gap that stays open is not announced at every dispatch). `dispatchAll` gives each watch one turn, so a storm on one watch does not delay another.
- **Event text is data.** The item is built from structured fields by the controller: kinds, records, keys, counts, reasons. Text that came with a signal is kept in a separate record, redacted and cut to 300 characters at write and again at read, and appears only under `data`, with `untrusted: true` and a note that it is not an instruction. Inbox admission turns only assignment messages into attempts, so an event item is `ignored` there: it cannot start work, change an assignment or a grant (tested, including a secret-shaped string inside the text). Event kinds are bounded words (H06), never free text.
- **Failures.** Delivery to the inbox that fails is recorded on the handling (error redacted and cut), retried by the next dispatch after a backoff of 30 s doubling, five attempts at most, then `dead`: the stream moves on and the next batch carries `reconcile: true` ("an earlier batch could not be delivered"). The owning agent (or the owner, or a service) `settle`s a handling `completed`, `failed` (kept listed) or `abandoned`, and `retry`s a failed or dead one as a new round (a new inbox item). `failures` lists them and `inspect` shows one (its events, the untrusted text, the inbox item's state), for the owner, the owning agent and anyone with `history.read` in the project; unreadable reads like missing. The same two reads are API operations (chat clients use the same operations).
- **Does not do.** It does not start the agent: the inbox item is what the worker's dispatcher picks up (the dispatcher, the pause service that supplies the `held` callback, and H03 admission of the resulting attempt are not wired here). It does not settle the inbox message (the S04 `settle` applies to admitted messages; the handling record carries the outcome). Real probes (a filesystem watcher, Git, service pings, model health) are not here. Source and watch records are never deleted.
- **Limits stated.** Loops are held where H13's sources publish them: an event suppressed by H06 that came from another publisher is stored as suppressed (and never delivered) but not turned into a held-loop record. A cause can only be an event of the same stream, so a change attributed to a task is linked only when the task's open handling is of the same stream as the signal (use the project stream for both); a cause not yet recorded when its dependent is published leaves the dependent unlinked (published once, never relinked). `failures` and `loops` scan at most 20 pages of 200 records per call (continue with `after`). A task's open-handling count is one record per owner under a revision check (concurrent dispatches for one owner retry). A stale source save (two runs at once) is ignored, which is safe because every published signal is recognised again. A watch is for one task; the stream is shared by other watches, each with its own cursor. The unowned check relies on the released assignment having no owner.

**Review round 1 changes.**
- **The agent-side consumer lives in H13 as an interface; the real scheduling is wired by H12 (lead decision).** The reconciler (`domain/reconcile.ts`) now skips inbox items whose source is `events` (`EVENTS_INBOX_SOURCE`), so it no longer marks them ignored; direct `admitAssignment` on one still answers `ignored`. `WatchService.consumeWakes(by, waker)` (owner or service) takes each scheduled handling that has not woken its agent and, when the stream is not on hold and the task's assignment is still held by the handling's owner **with an active attempt**, calls the injected `Waker.wake({agent, taskId, attemptId, handlingId, round, messageId, reconcile, events})`. A request is ids and counts only: no event text, no command. `woken` records the wake on the handling (once per round); `deferred` or no active attempt leaves it for a later call. Tested with a stub waker; no real scheduler exists until H12 provides one over admission. Scans at most 20 pages of 200 handlings per call (`scanLimited`).
- **Lease and cap (lead decision: 15 minutes default, `leaseSeconds` per service, owner-settable).** A scheduled handling counts against its owner (two) until settled, and its lease (default 900 s, `extendLease` up to a day) ends it: the next dispatch for that owner fails it as `failure.by: "lease"` and frees the place. The count is one record per owner holding the open handlings with their lease ends, so expiry costs no scan. A handling the agent reported `failed` no longer counts. `retry` needs room under the cap and is refused after five rounds in all. A batch waiting out a delivery backoff goes to the task's owner of the moment (the handling and its place move to the new owner; a task with no owner answers `unowned`).
- **Attribution.** The origin pointer uses the last event actually handed to the task, and is not opened for a handling with no events (filtered, suppressed, held-loop and missing sequence numbers are never what a change is attributed to). A missing sequence number was not tested directly (a hole cannot be made through the services); the filtered-kind case is.
- **Reconcile reasons** beyond ten are cut to nine plus "and N more"; the digest covers all of them. **Causes:** timers, task completion, services, models and snapshots all accept `causedByDedup` and `originTask` (one test over every source).
- **API.** `events.failures` is in record ID order (IDs are derived, so not chronological); non-owners see what they may read (the project filter narrows, it grants nothing); `failures` and `loops` carry `scanLimited` when the scan stopped early. The dead-path acknowledgement now has its own command ID (`-n`, success path `-w`), and dispatch retries only a stale revision, not a deterministic `command_conflict`.
- **Decisions recorded.** Only the owner releases a held loop for now; per-run source cap 200 (max 1000).

**Review round 2 changes.** The wake consumer no longer scans handlings: each owner's open-handling record marks the handlings that are scheduled and not yet woken (`waiting`), and `consumeWakes` reads only those records (one per agent) and then the waiting handlings in them, so settled handlings are never walked and deferred ones do not use up `limit` (tested with a spy on storage listing and with a first handling deferred under `limit: 1`). A lease can be extended at most four times and never past a day after scheduling; a lease-expired handling can still be settled `completed` (recorded as `late: true`). `failures`, `loops` and the consumer take `scanPages` and `scanPageSize` options (defaults 20 and 200) so the `scanLimited` marker is tested. Lead decisions: default lease 900 s and maximum extension 86,400 s; waking only an existing active attempt is intended (starting an attempt belongs to H12): a task with an owner but no attempt waits until the lease ends and then fails visibly as `lease`.

**H13 and idle expiry (H06b), after merging main.** Each dispatch of a watch first checks the watch's subscription: one that was idle-expired is resumed by the service (it keeps its cursor, so no event is lost) and the resume is recorded on the handling (`resumed: { at, cursor }`); otherwise the dispatch sends a heartbeat when the last activity is three quarters of the idle time old (a pull alone is not activity). A subscription that expires between the check and the pull is resumed and pulled again. Tested with clock advances of hours (dispatching every ten minutes for three hours never expires it; five hours of silence expires it and the next dispatch resumes it and delivers the waiting event). One small shared change: `CommandRunner.command` now answers a storage `idempotency_conflict` (the same command committed at the same moment, seen on PostgreSQL) with the recorded result, like it already did for a revision conflict, instead of reporting `command_conflict`; this removed a flaky concurrency test (15 of 15 runs clean, 3 of 12 failed before).

## G09 notes: proof of retained-context inquiry and mid-generation interruption (2026-10-22)

Evidence record: `docs/nimblenewt/implementation-plan/g09-evidence.md` (acceptance sentence to test mapping, exact manifests, mutation checks, what is not shown). Tests: `packages/integration/src/g09-retained-context.test.ts` with glue `g09-fixture.ts` (5 scenarios on each of the 4 controller backends, real Pi adapter on JSONL storage with the scripted offline provider), `packages/pi-adapter/src/model-change.test.ts`, and an extended `continuation.test.ts`.

- **Small changes to production code, all test-covered.** (1) C04 `ContinuationService.resume` takes an optional `restoredBy` (`native-restore`, `reconstruction`, `restart`), stored on the resume record, returned, part of the command's identity, refused if not one of the three; absent when the host does not say (the service never infers it). The card's "the resume record labels native restore, reconstruction or restart" was not satisfied by the existing `after` field (`restart`, `model-change`, `continue`), which says why a resume happened, not how the context came back. (2) The Pi adapter had no way to change a conversation's model: after a restart with another provider the stored root still named the old one and every request ended `unanswered/no_model`. `AdapterConversation.useModel(model)` applies Pi's own `configure({ model })` to that conversation (history untouched, nothing summarised). (3) `ContinuationService` and its types are now exported from the controller package index (it was missing); `@nimblenewt/controller/testing` re-exports the shared `backends` and `stopServer` so the integration package can run the same four backends.
- **Findings.** `useModel` applies to one conversation only: branches and tasks keep the model they were created with, so a controller that changes provider must move each live conversation. No audit event is emitted for it and it is not on `ConversationPort` yet; add both when the controller starts changing providers (the C04 resume record is the controller-side record today). The adapter exposes no stream events, so L05's chunk recording needs a relay; the fixture relays the partial call, and the integration of a real dispatcher is H12/T01.
- **How the proof is wired.** One inquiry is three records of the same boundary: the H04 inquiry, the C05 branch (opened with the `inquiryId`), and the Pi fork whose request ID is the branch ID. The fixture's `Mirror` writes the adapter transcript's text into the task history so the controller's inherited history and the adapter's forked context can be compared line for line. The adapter tool is the gate for actions: it records the complete call, then goes through `admitEffect` and `execute`, so an interrupted, corrected, stale or held generation produces a refusal the model sees as a tool result.
- **Limits.** The reconstruction case is a fixture that replays mirrored history as one instruction; there is no production reconstruction and Pi has no portable history export. The host rule "checkpoint verifies on the reopened storage means native restore" is the fixture's, not a service. The evidence is for the working tree; the commit hash is added when the card is committed.
- **Lead decisions.** `restoredBy` stays optional. The label is the host's word until H12 owns resume. `useModel` stays an explicit adapter method with no audit event for now; a controller that starts changing providers must audit it (a limit for H12/R04). Also: the L05 and adapter halves of the partial-call test are side by side, not wired (the L05 chunk is a stand-in), and no production code yet carries a C05 resume or applyUpdates result into the adapter conversation (test glue does), so "applied once" joins C05 dedupe and the adapter's request-ID dedupe.
- The real tests take the account signed in at the console as owner and share account (the runner's own account is only the fallback), so a pass cannot show that the narrow grants are what make access work when that account is also the runner's; a run with a separate runner account is needed for that claim.

**A03b first real run follow-up (2026-10-23).** The first run on the owner's machine failed 6 of 7 tests and the detail stayed on the machine. A failing real test now also writes `detail.json` (artifact `sandbox-windows-detail`, 7 days): the error, the failing step and the last 12 host calls with output cut to 2 KiB, passed through `windows-redact.ts` (accounts, domains, machine names, home directories, UNC servers and SIDs become `<user>`, `<host>`, `<home>`, `<sid>`; the cut is made after redaction; auto-login and lock-task results are never copied). The facts test reports each sub-fact by name as a boolean in `summary.json`. Suspected causes being checked: the runner account cannot see files under WindowsApps or list packages (admin rights), the console user is not the runner account (now the default owner), and another session's user name can be refused to an ordinary account (the session probe now falls back to WMI).

**A03b redaction: a limit to remember (2026-10-23).** The redaction of the Windows findings depends on which names it knows (the declared owner, the environment's names, the console user and its full name, an e-mail in the same text). If the console-user and full-name lookups both fail, only the generic rules (home folders, access lists, e-mail, SID, IP address) protect the owner's name, and a final gate withholds any field that still holds a known name. Treat the first artifacts as the real test and read them before sharing further.


## L01 notes: durable Pause gates and background settlement (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/lifecycle/` (`PauseService`, `PauseRefused`). Only the Pause mode exists; Suspend, Suspend and Release, Stop and Force Stop are L02 and L03 and will add modes and observed states (the observed-state names are already the full list from the operational contract).

- **Records.** `pause-gate` (one per scope, ID derived from the scope; created active, lazily, by the first check against the scope, never deleted), `pause-request` (immutable, one per pause or resume, numbered per gate) and `pause-background` (one per recorded background result). A request records actor, scope, mode (`pause`), action (`pause` or `resume`), the caller's operation ID (its command ID), the policy revision in force (installation and, for a task, project revision of `approval-policy`; 0 if never set) and the reason. The gate keeps `intent` (`active | pause`), who paused (`owner`), when, why, the `background` policy and the observation. The assignment, the task and the L05 generation gate are never written by a pause.
- **Intent and observation are separate.** A pause sets `observed: pausing` at once. `observe` records what the executor reports (idle, running, pausing, paused, preparing-to-suspend, suspended, stopping, stopped, recovery-required, unreachable). `paused` is accepted only with a safe-boundary statement (`finished`, or `cancelled_recorded`), never with `uncooperative`, and for a task scope never while its latest generation is still streaming (read from the L05 records); the answer then is to stay `pausing` or report `recovery-required` / `unreachable`. Time promotes nothing (tested with a day passing). Whoever may control the scope, the owner, or the agent's own runtime (for its agent or its task) may report. Resume clears the observation with the intent.
- **Scopes.** task, agent, team, installation. The gates that apply to a task are its own, its assignee agent's (assignment owner of kind `agent`), the agent's team and parent teams (walk of `parentTeamId`, bounded at 64, cycle-safe) and the installation's. A task-level pause blocks that task only (a response or meeting task of the same agent is free, and finishing it does not restart the paused one); an agent-level pause blocks all the agent's tasks. `resume` lifts that one gate and returns `stillPausedBy` (the gates above it that still hold). Nothing is written to descendants. Authority: task scope `assignment.manage` on the task; agent and team `agent.manage` (project `*`); installation the owner only. Unreadable targets read like missing ones; reasons refuse credential-shaped text; unknown fields (for example a `returnAt`) are refused.
- **No automatic return.** The gate has no return field of any kind. Elapsed time, a meeting, an incoming message and an applied configuration (U10: tested with an installation and an agent layer applied and the effective view read) do not write the gate (tested by its revision not moving). Only `resume` lifts it. A temporary meeting interruption with a return condition is a different mechanism that does not exist yet and must not reuse this gate.
- **Enforcement.** `guard(taskId)` returns the paused gates and the revision checks of every gate on the chain; a gate that does not exist yet is created active in the same commit, so a pause racing an admission is strictly before or after it (tested by a seam that pauses between check and claim, with no gate existing before, and by concurrent admit/pause and begin/pause rounds). Wired in: `GenerationService` (new optional dep `pauses`: `begin`, `admitEffect`, `execute`'s `beforeStart` and `guardFor` refuse with `GenerationRefused` reason `paused`, new), `PauseService.guardFor(taskId)` for any other `EffectService.run` starter (throws `PauseRefused`), `Reconciler` (new optional dep `pauses`: an inbox message for a paused task stays `received`, counted as `inboxHeld`, and is admitted by the first pass after the resume). An effect that already started is not interrupted by a pause (S05 rules; `beforeStart` runs only for unstarted effects).
- **Reconciling with L05's hold and the `manual_hold` blocker (decision).** The three stay separate and independent. `generation-gate.hold` (L05) remains the response-level control (cut the current response, or let it finish, then hold); it is not replaced and `interrupt`/`release` still work as before. The pause gate is the durable lifecycle gate; `GenerationService` consults both, so either blocks. (Lead decision: three separate holds are accepted because every mismatch fails safe: resuming one while another holds leaves the work blocked.) A pause does not cut a streaming response: the current step runs to a safe boundary, and a controller that wants it cut calls `interrupt(cancel)` as well (it should call `ConversationPort.abort()` as L05 says). `manual_hold` blockers are still honoured by the reconciler as before, are not created or lifted by a pause, and resolving one does not lift a pause (tested). Unifying them into one gate is possible later but would change what a reader of the blocker list sees; I did not do it.
- **Background results.** `recordBackground` records a finished background job for a task (job reference, outcome, redacted summary, the gates in force) whether or not it is paused, and may answer the typed wait it resolves (S08) in a second, idempotent command derived from the record (a crash between the two is repaired by repeating the call). No gate is lifted: the task becomes ready and stays unstartable. A pause may say `background: deny`, which refuses results until it is resumed. Allowed to the owner, `assignment.manage`, `attempt.work`, or the assigned agent. Results are read by ID only (`background`); there is no list.
- **Availability hook (T05).** `PauseService.availability` is the function `ConversationService` takes as `availability`: `paused` when the agent's own, its team chain's or the installation's gate is paused (so its inbound messages are queued and it cannot send), else the optional `otherAvailability` (for L02/L03's suspended and stopped), else `available`. A task-level pause leaves the agent available. Conversation code never writes a gate, so an incoming message cannot lift a pause (tested: gate revision unchanged). `api/start.ts` takes `availability` as an option and is not wired to this service by default; whoever composes the controller must pass `pauses.availability`.
- **Keeps its tasks.** `assertRetained(taskId, trigger)` (`idle_replacement | urgent_deadline | elapsed_time`) refuses while any gate on the chain is paused. Nothing in the repository reassigns automatically today (V01's takeover is `permitted: false`), so nothing calls it yet; any future automatic replacer must. Explicit release and emergency recovery are not decided here. V01: `StallService` takes `isPaused` (wire `pauses.isPaused`); a paused task is assessed `paused` (new status), has no concerns, and a suspected concern on it is cleared.
- **Changes outside the package.** `generation.ts` (dep, `paused` reason, four checks), `reconcile.ts` (optional `pauses`), `health/assess.ts` and `stall.ts` (`paused`), `domain/ids.ts` (prefixes `pgt`, `prq`, `pbg`). All covered by `pause.test.ts`.
- **Limits.** (1) Nothing here talks to the runtime: reporting `pausing`/`paused` is the executor's job (`observe`); an uncooperative tool is only reported as such. For agent, team and installation scopes `paused` rests on the reporter's boundary statement (only a task scope is cross-checked against L05's streaming state). (2) Every check reads the chain (task, assignment, agent, up to 64 teams, installation) and touches one gate record per level; first use creates up to that many empty active gates. (3) The gate carries a count of requests, the requests are not indexed beyond that (read by sequence).
