(4) Pausing a team does not write to its agents; moving an agent between teams changes which gates reach it at once. (5) A task credential (A10b) cannot pause or resume; the scope of a pause on a task whose assignee changes follows the new assignee (the task gate stays). (6) Resume by anyone with the scope's authority, not only the pauser; the pauser is recorded. (7) No owner notification or outbox message announces a pause or resume. (8) A pause of a done or cancelled task is refused; a resume is not.
- **For later cards.** L02/L03 should add modes to the same gate and request records, keep `observe` as the one place observed state is written, and make Suspend/Stop imply the pause check. L04 resume should go through `resume` and then bump the context revision with `correct`. G09 can drive: stream, pause at agent scope, background result answering a wait, message queued, resume.

**L01 review round 1 changes.**
- **Answering a wait.** `answerWait` no longer carries a `resolverId`. The caller must be the wait's named resolver (the owner may answer on its behalf); recording a result does not let anyone answer a wait meant for someone else (tested: a manager is refused, the resolver and the owner succeed). Recording a background result is now for the owner, `assignment.manage`, or the assigned agent (plain `attempt.work` was dropped).
- **The chain is locked in the commit.** `guard()` adds revision checks for the assignment, the agent and every team it walked, besides the gates, so a reassignment to a paused agent or a move into a paused team racing a check makes the commit fail and be decided again (seam tests with `afterPauseRead` for `begin`, `admitEffect`; the "no gate existed before" case is now true because the gates of an unchecked task are absent). Gap: a task with no assignment record yet cannot be locked (absence cannot be checked), so a first assignment racing a check to a paused agent is not ordered; the agent's own gate still blocks everything it then starts.
- **Background: deny** reads every gate on the chain, creating absent ones as `guard()` does, and the gates commit with the result (race test with a seam).
- **Reconciler gap.** Its pause check is a plain read, because admission is the inbox's own command. An attempt may be admitted just as a pause lands; no foreground work starts from it, since `begin`, `admitEffect` and `execute` are guarded in their own commits (tested).
- **A pause is weaker than `manual_hold` for already-started effects.** The reconciler holds a started but unfinished effect of a task under `manual_hold`; under a pause it re-runs it (it settles an effect that already started; it starts nothing new). 
- **Wiring is left to the composer.** `api/start.ts` is not wired to `pauses.availability`, `StallService` needs `isPaused: pauses.isPaused` and `Reconciler` needs `pauses`; follow-up for H12 (the dispatcher) to pass `PauseService.guardFor` into every `EffectService.run` and compose these.
- **Lead decisions recorded.** Three separate holds are acceptable (each mismatch fails safe); a lifecycle pause reaches a safe boundary and does not cut the streaming response (the caller adds `interrupt(cancel)` if wanted); anyone with the scope's authority may resume; `background: deny` stays; agent and team scopes keep riding on `agent.manage` with project `*`.

**L01 review round 2.** Storage refuses an operation that names one record twice, and the fence guard and the pause chain lock both check the assignment, so fenced `begin`, `admitEffect` and `execute` failed once pauses were wired. `domain/changes.ts` `mergeChanges` now reduces the merged guard lists (generation `begin`, `admitEffect`, the execute guard, `guardFor`, and `EffectService.run`'s fence plus `beforeStart`) to one check per record; two checks of one record at different revisions are `stale_revision`. Tested by `domain/changes.test.ts` and a fenced pause test on all backends (removing the merge makes it fail).


## R04 notes: provider drain, cutover and failback policy (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/routing/cutover.ts` (`CutoverService`), `lists.ts` (topology, `checkLists`, `MODEL_USING_ROLES`), and changes to `fallback.ts` (R02) and `service.ts` (R01). Tests: `cutover.test.ts`, 40 per backend (160 on four backends), plus the R02 tests unchanged. New record kinds: `route-topology`, `route-controls`, `route-health`.

- **Fallback lists.** `RoutingService.withEpoch(..., rules)` (optional; without rules the table is judged as before) refuses a table unless every model-using role has a valid list: `coordinator` and `safety-supervisor` always, plus every role the table names. Valid means a profile of routes (never another list, so a fallback has no list of its own to follow and cycles cannot arise in lists), no two entries that are the same route under different aliases, at least two entries not all on one endpoint-and-account; `acknowledgedWeak` (owner choice, per role) accepts fewer. `setTopology` re-checks the lists, and `setTable` re-checks the topology, so neither can quietly break the other. Alias chains, cycles and self-aliases are refused.
- **Topology.** The owner records, per canonical route, `endpoint`, `account` and optional local `service`, the local `services` that may be switched off (whether or not a route uses them), and `aliases` (alias route to canonical route, one step). An unmapped route is its own provider's endpoint and account. Names are plain names (not URLs); nothing here probes or discovers.
- **Controls.** `disable`, `drain`, `cutover`, `reenable` at levels `model`, `endpoint`, `account`, `provider`, `service`; aliases are resolved (a model-level control on an alias is stored as the route it names; an alias of a disabled provider or of a route R01 excludes is blocked too). Modes only go disabled -> drain -> cutover. Owner (or `routing.write`) only. Controls block every new dispatch from the commit on, because the fallback service asks the gate for every candidate (first attempt, fallback, retry, `resume`) and, after each dispatch commits, asks again: if a control landed in between, the attempt is abandoned and the next route chosen (so no request is sent to a route after its control committed).
- **Drain and report.** `drain` needs an explicit `drainSeconds` (no default: the decision says drain duration is unset). `report(level, name)` gives three separate facts: `routingApplied` (a control is committed), `inFlightRemaining` (calls whose current attempt is on the target) and `safeToStop` (both). With no route using the target (cloud-only installation, a service nothing maps to) it reports applied, 0, safe. `drainDeadlinePassed` is information: nothing cancels automatically; the owner issues `cutover`. `stopDiscardsCache` is true for local services.
- **Cutover.** Commits the control, then abandons each in-flight attempt on the target (`FallbackService.abandon`: outcome `abandoned`, not a failure, no cooldown or provider block); the call moves to the next eligible route, or waits if an effect is uncertain (nothing is repeated). Repeating the cutover command re-sweeps (use after a crash). The cancelled attempts are passed to the listener given to `bind` (for the runtime abort and L05 `interrupt`; see below). **Late responses:** `succeeded`/`failed` take an optional `attempt`; a report for an attempt that is no longer current is recorded under `late` (cap 20), applies nothing (`succeeded` returns `{applied:false}`), but its cost is counted in the task spend. A call that has abandoned an attempt refuses reports that do not name theirs. **Possible charges:** abandoning an attempt on a paid provider records a `possible` charge on the call; a late report with usage confirms it; the owner settles it with `settleCharge` (`none` or `confirmed` with cost); the report counts the open ones.
- **Health and failback.** An availability failure reported through the fallback service marks a scope: credit/auth -> the whole `account`; outage -> the `endpoint`; rate limit -> that `model` for 60 s (no probes). Task errors, bad answers (invalid request, unknown) and context overflows teach the gate nothing and R02 already fails the call instead of failing over. A down scope blocks new work (reason in the wait list) until the failback policy returns it, or until a half-open trial (below): default `automatic`, 3 good probes spread over at least 120 s, probes by a dispatcher (`probe`); a bad probe restarts the count; a failure within `holdDownSeconds` (900) of a restore marks it `needsOwner`, and then only `restore` (owner) brings it back. `manual` never restores by probes. Probes never touch controls: a disabled route stays disabled until `reenable`. Running calls are not moved back; new work starts at the top of its list.
- **List position.** Each call stores `list` (profile, resolved ordered routes, index of the current attempt), refreshed at every attempt, with the full attempt history; a restart reads it from storage (tests rebuild all services on the same storage).
- **Cache.** Nothing here issues an eviction, flush or cache-clearing call to any engine, and moving new work to another engine touches only NimbleNewt's own call records; the report says stopping a local service discards that engine's in-memory cache. Tracking which conversations are warm on which engine (C06) is not built, so the controller cannot yet warn that a particular cached conversation will be lost.
- **Limits, honestly.** (1) The service records and fences; the dispatcher must actually abort the HTTP request / runtime and call L05 `GenerationService.interrupt(cancel)` for the cancelled tasks (the `bind` listener is the place); not wired here. (2) Health is only as good as the failures and probes the dispatcher reports; with `automatic` and no probe sender a failed endpoint/account stays down until the owner restores it (a half-open trial is not built). (3) `report` and cutover scan every model-call record (200 per page); fine for hundreds of calls, wants an index of open calls at scale. (4) A drain deadline is never enforced by itself. (5) An in-flight attempt started before a control committed is seen by the sweep; one still being committed is caught by the post-commit check, but a dispatcher that sends the request before calling `begin`/`step` back is outside this. (6) Health of a model-scope rate limit uses the fixed R02 60 s. (7) Topology entries naming a route the table later drops block the table change (by design); names with `/` are hashed in health IDs. (8) Alias resolution is one step and only for routes in the table.
- **Decisions made (owner may revisit).** Default failback is automatic (3 probes, 120 s, hold-down 900 s) rather than manual; a list needs two endpoint-distinct entries unless acknowledged; model-using roles default to coordinator and safety-supervisor; drain has no default duration; `RoutingService`'s new rules are opt-in (`api/start.ts` now passes `{}`: the defaults, coordinator and safety-supervisor plus every role in the table, are enforced for the Controller's routing service and so for `ConfigurationService.bindRouting`; an installation that wants other required roles or acknowledgements passes a rules object there).

**Review round (2026-10-22), changes and lead decisions.**
- **Concurrency.** Health writes meet on one record; `#failure` re-runs the command on a revision conflict (up to 8 times) and a gate error never fails `failed()` (`#learn` swallows it). The health command payload is the reported failure (route, class, time), not the topology, so an old report replayed after a topology change is the recorded answer. Tested with six concurrent `failed` on all backends.
- **Unsent attempts.** `FallbackService.sent(callId, attempt)` is the dispatcher's "about to send". The post-commit re-check, and `step()` (so also after a restart), take back an attempt that was not marked sent if its route is blocked now: it stays in the attempt list as `skipped` (attempt numbers are never reused, so two skips in a row work and a late response naming a skipped attempt is recorded as late and never applied to the replacement), records no charge and does not count toward the four attempts (the limit counts non-skipped attempts). A sent attempt is left alone by a disable or drain; only a cutover cancels it. A dispatcher that never calls `sent` has every attempt re-checked at each `step`.
- **Cutover delivery.** The control carries `cancelPending` until every attempt it abandoned was passed to the `bind` listener and the cancellation acknowledged. The sweep goes on past a call that fails (per-call retry on conflict), still notifies for what was cancelled and rethrows afterwards; a repeat of the command (or any `tick`, `report` by a dispatcher, or `step`) retries and re-notifies all attempts that cutover abandoned, so a listener must be idempotent. `report.safeToStop` is false while cancellation is pending. `cutover` checks the fallback service is bound before committing anything. `report.routingApplied` also sees a control at an enclosing level that covers every route of the target (a provider cutover seen from its service).
- **Late spend.** A late report's cost is counted only when its attempt has no charge entry or the charge is still `possible` (a settled or already confirmed charge is not counted again).
- **Gate writes.** The gate (health writes, trial claim, drain expiry) and the trial claim write their own records from inside another command's build; they survive if the outer command is then refused. They are bounded (one health record per scope, one trial per window) and idempotent by derived command IDs.
- **Half-open.** A down account/endpoint records `retryAt` (failure time plus the R02 outage cooldown, 300 s). From then, in automatic mode and unless it needs the owner, one call may use it. It is claimed (by a revision race) only for the route the call actually chooses, after the cooling and already-tried checks; success returns the target (and starts the hold-down clock); failure sets the next `retryAt`. So with nothing probing, one transient outage costs about five minutes, not an owner action. Manual mode has no trial.
- **Drain deadline.** The owner's `drainSeconds` is the bound: a drain past it becomes a cutover recorded as "drain deadline reached" (`expired`), applied lazily by the next gate decision, `step`, `report` or `tick(by)` (a scheduler can call `tick`), then swept like any cutover. The sweep needs a dispatcher principal (`tick`, `step`); a gate decision alone only flips the mode (new work was already blocked by the drain).
- **Lists.** A list needs two distinct endpoints, and two distinct accounts unless every entry is a local engine (nominal accounts of local engines may be shared) or the owner acknowledged the role.
- **Configuration setting.** `routing.fallbackProfile.*` (settings.ts) stays as it is. It is superseded by the per-role lists of R04 (a list entry is a route and cannot be another list); a later configuration card should retire or map it. It is not read by routing today.
- **Still open.** The listener (runtime abort and L05 `interrupt`) is not wired in the Controller; `CutoverService` and `FallbackService` are not constructed by `api/start.ts`; scans read every call record.

## H05b notes: reviewer pause and hold behaviour (2026-10-22)

The lifecycle half of H05, in `packages/controller/src/review/review.ts` (`ReviewService`, three new options, one new read `wakeList`, changes to `sweep`, `decide` and reviewer choice) and `routing.ts` (`ReviewerStatus`, `ReviewRouteBody.reviewerStatus`). 9 tests per backend (36 runs) in `review/hold.test.ts`; the 72 routing and 36 review tests are unchanged and still pass.

- **Wiring.** `ReviewService.withEpoch(storage, epoch, { availability, reviewerHost, hostStaleAfterMs })`. `availability` is L01's `PauseService.availability` (or anything with the `Availability` type: `paused`, `suspended`, `stopped`, so L02 and L03 need no change here). Whoever composes the controller must pass it, as with `ConversationService` (the default is "everyone is available", which is the old behaviour). `api/start.ts` is not wired.
- **Held reviewer.** Held means the reviewer agent's own gate, its team chain's or the installation's is paused (or `otherAvailability` says suspended or stopped). A **task-level** pause of some other task of the reviewer does not hold the reviewer (same rule as L01's availability; tested). The gate's intent is enough: a reviewer in `pausing` is already held, so a reviewer that is finishing its step is not woken for more; it reports `paused` at its own safe boundary with `PauseService.observe` and nothing else is needed from this service.
- **Keeps its place.** The review stays `pending`, assigned to the held reviewer, with its original deadline (the deadline does not stop while held, and resuming does not extend it). A sweep before the deadline records `reviewerStatus: "held"` and one `held` event and moves nothing; repeating the sweep writes nothing. When the reviewer is resumed the next sweep records `resumed` and clears the status. No pause/resume call reaches this service; the status is a record of what the last sweep saw, and every decision reads availability itself.
- **Never woken, never chosen, never deciding.** Reviewer choice (`request`, `sweep`, `addContributor`) skips held reviewers (reason `is paused` and so on in the waiting event). `wakeList(by, projectId)` (needs `review.policy.write`, like `sweep`; the owner too) returns the pending assigned reviews whose reviewer may be woken (`wake`) and those that may not (`held`, with `why`: `paused`, `suspended`, `stopped` or `host-lost`); the dispatcher wakes only the first list. A held reviewer's `decide` is refused (`invalid_command`, field `reviewer`); the owner is never refused.
- **Moves on at the deadline, never by approval.** At the deadline the existing H05 escalation applies: another eligible reviewer, the authorized fallbacks, then the owner (`owner-decision`, which only the owner decides). The held reviewer is added to `tried`, so resuming it does not give the review back. Nothing in the sweep ever sets `approved` or a verdict (tested: held reviewer, then held fallback, then the owner; the review is never approved by the passing of time). With no one eligible a waiting review waits, and goes to the owner at its deadline.
- **Lost host versus slow reviewer (V01's distinction).** The reviewer's host is read from `reviewerHost(reviewer)` (nothing records which host runs an agent before H03, so the composer supplies it). Only a **revoked host identity** is `host-lost`, a fact that does not depend on a clock; the review then moves to the next eligible reviewer at the next sweep, without waiting for the deadline, and a lost host is not chosen for new reviews. A host that stopped heartbeating (`stale`) is only `host-silent`: a partition looks the same, so it is noted once and the deadline stands, exactly like a slow reviewer on a live host. A host never heard from, or no host given, is not judged. If a lost-host reviewer is the only one, the review keeps its place, is flagged `host-lost` and goes to the owner at the deadline. Held takes precedence over the host: a held reviewer on a lost host still keeps the review until the deadline (the hold was a deliberate act, and moving early would contradict "keeps its place").
- **Changes outside the card.** None to other packages. `sweep`'s result shape is unchanged (flag changes are not listed in it; read `getRoute` or `wakeList`); the sweep command's detail gains a `noted` count.
- **Limits.** (1) Availability is read when a review is routed, swept or decided, not inside the commit; a pause that lands between choosing a reviewer and the commit leaves the review assigned to a reviewer that is already held, which is harmless (it is not woken and does not decide, and the sweep notes it). The pause gate's revisions are not locked in review commits. (2) The held/host status is only as fresh as the last sweep (no timer drives it yet), but `decide` and `wakeList` read live. (3) Only agent reviewers are ever held; the owner is not. (4) The deadline is the policy's deadline; the owner is not asked anything about a held reviewer (the project policy decides). (5) `reviewerHost` is a function, not a record: until H03 links workers to hosts a composer decides what it means. (6) `wakeList` returns at most 200 entries and says `more`. (7) A reviewer paused mid-review whose pause is long-lived delays the review by up to the deadline (60 minutes by default); there is no "move on as soon as it is held" mode, by decision.
- **For later cards.** The dispatcher (H12) should call `wakeList` and wake only `wake`, pass `pauses.availability` and a host lookup to `ReviewService`, and drive `sweep` on a schedule. L02/L03 modes need nothing here if `otherAvailability` reports them.

**H05b lead decisions (after review).** The L01 reading is confirmed: a pause of one task of a reviewer does not hold the reviewer. A held reviewer's review waits for the deadline (an option to move it on at once is a later owner setting). The hold takes precedence over a revoked host. A sweep now handles at most 49 reviews (each takes two changes and a storage operation allows 100; the old cap of 50 would have failed with 101 changes when a policy exists), and at the cap a status note sets `more`, so a scheduler keeps going; an operation reserved for the owner after routing drops the held status with the assignment.
## U03 notes: owner chat command interpretation (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/chat/` (`ChatService`, `CoordinatorService`, `ScriptedInterpreter`, the operation table in `commands.ts`), two test files (`chat.test.ts` 23 tests, `coordinator.test.ts` 10 tests, each on all four backends; mutation-checked: authority check off, quoted basis ignored, cited words unchecked, proposal mode off, draft flag ignored, same-message apply allowed, a question not holding the message, pins unchecked, owner check off, ambiguity resolved by guessing, no predicted revision, a missing repository ignored, forged citations accepted, plan cycles accepted, coordinator state ignored, assignments always delegated, route bound removed, proposal owner unchecked, quoted lines not split, credentials accepted; one survives by design: the proposal-owner check inside apply; after review, further mutations on the new rules are listed in the review follow-up: foreign values, weak evidence, authority fields, `$ref`, repository-step revision, running-task repository, idle pin, permissive default profile, refused-comes-back, second answer, choice unchecked, principal comparison, scoped owner, assignment without grant).

- **Never executed from model text.** The model call sits behind `ChatInterpreter.interpret(input)`; tests use `ScriptedInterpreter`. Its answer is untrusted data: `checkIntents` validates it field by field (a malformed answer is refused whole, `interpreter_invalid`) into a small vocabulary: `command` (a state-API operation name and arguments), `clarify`, `switch_focus`, `apply_proposal`, `discard_proposal`, `none`. A command must be one of the sixteen chat operations (`CHAT_OPS`: project, task, assignment, contract, dependency, agent, team and role records), its argument names must be ones the API's own operation table lists, and what the owner left out is filled from records, not from the model: a task or project named by title or name (exactly one match, else a question), the project in focus, and the revision to expect (so "rename the task" needs no revision; a revision the owner did quote is kept and the API's stale check stands).
- **Same door.** An applied command is a `StateApi.handle` request under the owner's own credential, with command ID `<turn>-c<i>`. Authorization, revision checks, idempotent replay and accepted-versus-completed come from the API unchanged; a refusal is reported with its API code. Only the owner talks to the chat (an agent, client or task credential is `forbidden`).
- **Authority from the owner's words only.** The message is split into the owner's own text and quoted material (`>` lines, fenced blocks, and whatever the client passes as `quoted`). The interpreter sees them apart; an intent with `basis: "quoted"`, or whose `evidence` (the owner's words it rests on) is not found in the owner's own text, is refused as `untrusted_authority`. A credential-shaped owner message is refused unread; credential-shaped quoted text is withheld from the interpreter. This is a check of the *source* of an instruction, not of its content, so it is backed by three more rules that do not trust the model: evidence must be a phrase (two words, six characters) found in the owner's words; every text value of a command (titles, names, descriptions, roles, capabilities, ...) must itself appear in the owner's words, except record references (task, project, agent, team and repository IDs, which the API checks against records) and fixed choices (`to`, `state`, `workClass`, ...), so a model cannot add content the owner never said; and fields that change what someone may do are proposals whatever the evidence (below). What remains possible for a fooled interpreter is to point an ordinary, authorized, reversible command at a record the owner did not name (an ID is accepted if it is a readable record); that is bounded by the API's authorization and the preview rules, not prevented.
- **Show before anything irreversible; the profile decides the rest (lead decision a, 2026-10-22).** Per the guiding principle an explicit owner order such as "assign X to Ann" or "mark X done" applies directly in the derived and autonomous profiles (read from `PolicyService.effective`; absent, unreadable or strict is a proposal). Always proposals, in every profile: a new project (a drafted project), anything the interpreter marks `draft`, role definitions, and any command that sets an authority-bearing field (`contract.set` permissions, budget, workClass; agent roles, supervisor, permittedOverrides, defaultProfile; project `addRepositories`; a task's `repositoryId`). In strict (or unreadable) also proposals: assign/transfer/release, agent retire, dependency remove, a move to `done`/`cancelled`, archiving. Reversible edits (task update, ready or in-progress moves, contract fields other than the above, agent and team creation or update, dependency add) always apply. A proposal is saved (`chat-proposal`) with typed steps with fixed command IDs, a one-line-per-step preview built from the typed command (never from model text), and pins. Nothing is admitted until `applyProposal` (or a later message whose interpretation is `apply_proposal`); a proposal is never applied by the message that drew it up. Applying twice, concurrently or after a crash does each step once (step command IDs are fixed) and the second answer is `replayed`.
- **One question holds the message, and the answer carries it on.** If any part needs an answer (ambiguity, no project in focus, no determinable repository, an interpreter `clarify`) nothing else in that message runs; those parts are `held`. The held commands, each with the words it was said in, are stored in the turn and the open question is kept in the owner's context and shown to the interpreter. The owner's answer (an `answer` intent naming the turn and one of the offered choices, with phrase evidence in the owner's own words) re-plans the held commands with the choice filled in, once; commands that were refused stay refused, a choice not offered or a second answer is refused, and a question the interpreter itself asked (`clarify`) is not resumable (the owner restates).
- **Plan first.** The resolved plan is stored (`chat-turn`, status `planned`) before the first command runs; a retry of the same `messageId` resumes from the stored plan without asking the model again, and a finished turn is returned as it was. The turn links every outcome to its command IDs (and so to the audit entries and proposal IDs). Several commands in one message that write the same record expect the revisions the earlier ones will leave.
- **Per-project context, focus and dispatch.** `chat-context` (per owner) holds the focus and, per project, the last task and the last twenty turns; switching focus keeps every project's thread. A title resolves only inside the focused project. A task handed to someone carries an explicit project and repository (lead decisions b and e): a task with no repository gets the project's only one set as a first step (applied or proposed together with the order), and with none or several the owner is asked (the answer is resumable); the repository ID stands in for the checkout until a checkout record exists. Giving a task a different repository is refused while it is in progress or assigned (`task_running`), and is otherwise a proposal that is refused again at apply if the task has started meanwhile. Those pins are rechecked at apply, so a later change of focus or of the task cannot retarget what was shown. `task.assign` still only says accepted: completion waits for the owner's worker.
- **Coordinator.** `CoordinatorService` keeps one `coordinator` record: its own principal (an agent with its own grants; the record grants nothing), a list of routes (primary then fallbacks, at most five, four tried per request, the R02 bound) and a lifecycle only the owner controls (set up `paused`; running, paused, stopped; stopped can only be started). `answer` searches the records the owner may read, shows only those to the model and accepts an answer only if it cites records it was shown; if records do not answer and a task is named it opens an inquiry (once per command ID) to whoever can answer, the agent named or else the task's assignee, never the owner and never itself; with nobody to ask it says the records are not enough. A coordinator task-scoped owner credential is refused like in chat, and the credential the controller holds must be the principal the owner configured, else the coordinator is unavailable. `assign` has the model pick agents for unassigned tasks (only tasks shown, each with a reason; a task with no repository is skipped): a coordinator whose own principal holds `assignment.manage` for the project makes these assignments itself under its credential without the owner's apply, the reason recorded in the assignment (lead decision c); without the grant they are a proposal. A plan that includes anything else (`decompose`: tasks, dependencies) always waits for the owner's apply. `decompose` validates the model's plan (at most 20 tasks, unique keys, known dependencies, no ring, repositories that exist) and saves task creations, dependencies and assignments as one proposal. Assignment steps are marked for the coordinator only if its own principal holds `assignment.manage` for the project (they then run under its credential when the owner applies); otherwise the owner's apply runs them. Tasks of a plan with no repository are not assigned (noted). Paused, stopped, not set up or all routes failing gives `unavailable`; chat, proposals and the API do not depend on it (tested).
- **`$ref`.** References between proposal steps are structural (`{ "$ref": "key" }`) and appear only in steps the controller or coordinator builds; an interpreter command carrying `$ref` anywhere in its arguments is refused, and the old text form `"$ref:x"` is plain text.
- **Decisions made.** Chat is owner-only; credential-shaped owner text is refused rather than redacted (lead decision d); an interpreter that is down records nothing (the same message can be sent later) while an unusable answer is recorded as a refused turn; the interpreter's evidence must be a substring of the owner's text (case and spacing ignored); `done`/`cancelled` moves and archiving follow the profile as above; a project may have no repository, but a dispatched task may not (this follows the card over the existing "a task never needs one"). The surviving proposal-owner mutant cannot be killed by a test: there is exactly one owner principal and no way to enroll a second.
- **Limits stated.** No real model integration and no transport (the chat endpoint, sessions and rendering are other cards; `reply` is plain lines). The coordinator's route list is plain names, not wired to the R01 routing table or the R02 fallback classification (any exception moves to the next route). The coordinator is not yet an `agent` record with a host and lifecycle in the supervisor sense (the agent record is H11, the host is L01): it has a principal and a record of its own. Pending revisions are predicted for four record kinds only, and a prediction that is wrong shows as the API's `stale_revision`. Title lookup reads at most five pages of 100. The "checkout" is the task's repository ID, since no checkout record exists yet. A proposal is not versioned: if a step is refused for staleness the owner asks again. Open proposals and per-owner context are scanned (at most 1000 proposal records) rather than indexed. Command namespaces and aliases (`/nimblenewt.*`) are T09, which will call into this.
- **Final moves need the owner's verb, and quoted text shows everything first (second review, lead decisions a+b).** (a) An irreversible or final move applies directly (in derived or autonomous) only if a sentence of the owner's own, non-quoted text contains a verb from a small fixed list and the target's name (task title, project name or agent name, whole words): to done/cancelled (done: done, finish, complete, close; cancelled: cancel, drop, abandon, each its own list), archived (archive), agent.retire (retire, fire, let go, remove), dependency.remove (remove, delete, drop, unlink), task.release (release, give up, let go), task.transfer (transfer, hand over, reassign, move), task.assign (assign, give, allocate), none satisfying another; a sentence ending in "?" never counts; otherwise a proposal. (b) When the message carried any quoted material, every command except plain edits (task update or create, project update without a state, agent update, contract fields) is a proposal. Tests for the three probe cases in strict, derived and autonomous, a verb in another sentence, an owner verb with quoted text, and the positive "cancel task Keep me" applying in derived. `CoordinatorService.assign` builds each assignment's command ID from the command ID and the task ID, and its proposal carries the same project and repository pins as chat dispatch proposals (rechecked at apply).

## P01 notes: repository-independent projects and responsibilities (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/projects/` (`ProjectResourceService`, `ProjectPlanService`, `ResponsibilityService`), two test files (`projects.test.ts` 21 tests and `chat.test.ts` 4 tests, each on all four backends). Small edits elsewhere: `ProjectBody` gained four optional planning fields (`domain/model.ts`), new error code `checkout_changed` (409 over HTTP), new derived-ID prefixes `rsc`/`rsp`, eight new action names in `ACTIONS`, eighteen new state-API operations (8 queries, 10 commands) (`api/operations.ts`, optional `resources`/`plans`/`responsibilities` dependencies of `StateApi`, wired in `start.ts` and the chat test harness) and three new chat operations (`resource.bind`, `project.plan`, `responsibility.create`).

- **A project needs no repository, and a repository is a binding, not an identity.** `bindResource` creates a `project-resource` record for a project: kind `repository | output | document | service | other`, a label (unique among the project's bound resources), an opaque locator (a path, URL or name; credential-shaped text is refused), an access scope and a location version (`locatorEpoch`). A repository binding also lists the repository in the project record (the S01 `repositories`, same operation), so tasks can name it, and binding one the project already lists must agree with its location. Non-code outputs and services are bound the same way and never appear as repositories. Uniqueness is serialized through a per-project registry record (the S10 pattern), so simultaneous binds cannot both take a label or handle.
- **Separate access scopes.** The scope belongs to the binding: `{ mode: read | write, paths }` (no paths means the whole resource). The same repository bound to two projects is two records with two scopes. `authorizeUse` answers whether a use (read or write, optionally of a path) is inside the scope; paths are checked as relative, `..`-free, drive-letter-free and stream-marker-free, compared exactly and case-sensitively (a differently cased path is refused, not guessed), and the whole resource is not "a path below" a path-limited scope. Read is the default; write, or any widening of a scope, needs `resource.scope.write` on top of `resource.write`; narrowing needs only `resource.write`. This is a record and a check the later tool path (T06, A04) can call; nothing here enforces it on a process.
- **Internal state stays outside target repositories.** Everything is a controller record; the services import no file-system module (a test greps the compiled files) and never read or write a locator. A test binds a real directory, moves, scopes and dispatches, and finds it empty.
- **A moved or same-named checkout does not redirect a task.** A task is dispatched to a binding by the project's handle for the repository (`repositoryId`), never by location, label or name. `pinCheckout` (needs `assignment.manage` for the task and `resource.read`) records the binding and its `locatorEpoch` in a `task-checkout` record; `resolveCheckout` returns the binding's location and scope for an undispatched task (not pinned) and, for a pinned one, only while the binding is the one it was pinned to, still bound, at the same location version, and the task still names that repository; otherwise `checkout_changed` (no redirect, nothing guessed). `repinCheckout` (revision-guarded) confirms the new state. Unbinding then binding another resource with the same label or location gives another handle and another record, so a same-named checkout captures nothing; handles are never reused. The same location in another project is another project's binding and is never consulted. This is the "checkout record" U03 left to a later card, in the minimal form the card needs: it is not yet wired into `task.assign`.
- **Optional planning on the project record.** `ProjectPlanService.setPlan` (`project.plan`, revision-guarded on the project) sets a due date (`YYYY-MM-DD`, due at the end of that UTC day, or a UTC timestamp; impossible dates refused), workstreams (at most 20), milestones (at most 50, each open or reached, optionally dated and in a workstream) and authorized participants (at most 50 active agent principals with an optional role). Each field given replaces that field; a field left out is kept; `dueDate: null` clears it; the S01 project commands keep these fields. The plan needs `project.plan.write`, the participant list `project.participants.write` (a command naming a field needs that field's right). A participant listing grants nothing and is not yet consulted by assignment.
- **Passing a due date changes nothing.** There is no timer or sweeper for dates. `schedule` computes `overdue` from the injected clock when asked and writes nothing (a test compares audit count and the project and task records before and after the clock moves a year); a milestone is `reached` only because a command said so.
- **Ongoing responsibilities.** `responsibility` records live in a project (so, with no repository): title, objective, desired condition, the answerable agent, the managed resources, check interval and event-source names, permitted actions (narrow, never grant), UTC maintenance windows, escalation (`owner` or an agent), and a state `active | paused | retired` (retired is final). Each check, intervention, incident or note is a `responsibility-event` entry (ID `<responsibility>.<seq>`, gap-free, append-only, optional outcome and a link to the bounded task it produced; credential-shaped text refused); `status` reports the last check, the next due time, whether a check is overdue and whether now is inside a maintenance window, and starts nothing. No model conversation is implied by a responsibility.
- **One managing responsibility per resource.** Managing a resource takes a claim (`resource-claim`, ID = the resource's); a second responsibility is refused while it is held, simultaneous claims have exactly one winner (the claim create or the revision guard fails the other; the loser is told the resource is managed elsewhere), dropping a resource from `managed` or retiring releases it, a managed resource cannot be unbound, and an unbind racing a claim fails one of them (both guard the binding's revision).
- **Authority.** `resource.read|write|scope.write`, `project.plan.write`, `project.participants.write`, `responsibility.read|write|assign`. Naming the answerable agent or the managed resources needs `responsibility.assign`. History may be added by the installation owner, the named owner agent or a holder of `responsibility.write`. Every read of something the caller may not see answers like a missing one, and refusals name no project. Chat: see the review follow-up below (the first version made every bind a proposal).
- **Decisions made.** A responsibility always belongs to a project (a standalone, project-less responsibility would need a project-less authorization model, which A01 does not have). A repository binding written by `bindResource` also changes the project record (one extra revision), so a later `project.update` in the same chat message sees a stale predicted revision and the API says so. A repository the project lists without a binding (added by `project.update` before this card) resolves for an undispatched task only after it is bound: `resolveCheckout` says "not bound". Unbinding is allowed while tasks are pinned to it (they then refuse with `checkout_changed`).
- **Limits.** The checkout is not yet wired into assignment (S02) or dispatch from chat (U03's pins still use the repository ID): a later card must call `pinCheckout` at assignment and `resolveCheckout` before a worker starts. Scope enforcement on tools and paths, participant checks at assignment, and acting on an overdue check or a maintenance window (scheduling, the event path of H13) are not here. Listing a project's bindings or responsibilities scans every record of the kind (like the S10 dependency edges) and refuses with `unavailable` beyond 5000 rather than answering from part; there is no secondary index. A history is read by sequence number, 100 at a time. Maintenance windows are UTC weekdays and times only (no time zones, no one-off windows); `everyMinutes` is a plain interval (no calendar schedules). A resource can be moved but its old location is not kept (the audit entry has the new epoch only).
- **Review follow-up (lead decisions a to e).** (1) `scopeAllows` now refuses an unsafe path (`../x`, `/abs`, `C:\\x`) whatever the scope, unlimited included. (2) A move needs `resource.scope.write` as well when the binding is writable or unlimited (a read-limited one moves with `resource.write`). (3) The registry record is read first in bind, move and unbind (the S10 pattern), so label checks cannot be raced; a test races four output binds with different locators and a mutant without the registry fails it. (4) A bind or move whose location contains, or lies inside, a location bound to another project is refused unless `acknowledgeOverlap` is given by someone holding `resource.scope.write` (equal locations stay allowed: one repository, two projects; the comparison is by normalized, case-folded path text, so symlinks and aliases are not seen). The refusal does reveal to the caller that such a binding exists. (5) Unbinding is refused while an unfinished task is pinned to the binding (the message names the tasks) and allowed after they finish; the repository's handle stays in the project's list (tasks may name it), but dispatch (`pinCheckout`, `resolveCheckout`) refuses an unbound one; `project.update` can only add repositories, so a bound location cannot be changed through it. A default repository handle made from the label steps aside (`app-2`) when an old one is used. (6) `record` refuses a task the caller cannot read exactly like one of another project. (7) Chat: a read-only bind of a location in the owner's own words follows the profile (direct in derived and autonomous when a sentence has a bind/add/attach/connect/link verb and the label; strict, quoted text, a location not in the owner's words, write access and `acknowledgeOverlap` make it a proposal); participants are always a proposal and may be named by `agentName`, resolved from records to exactly one active agent; a bind with no location asks, unless the records hold exactly one unbound binding of that label, whose location is then proposed (never applied unseen); a `project.update` or `project.plan` after a bind in the same message predicts the revision the bind leaves (for a repository not already in the project's list; if it was, the API reports `stale_revision`). Responsibility creation stays a proposal; a responsibility belongs to a project (installation-level ones are a later card); maintenance windows are UTC only. Chat has no unbind operation.
- **What the overlap guard does not see (second review).** Locators are never resolved on disk, so the guard compares spelling only: it collapses repeated separators and `.` segments, treats `file://` as a plain path, folds case, reads backslashes as separators, and refuses `..` segments and percent-escaped separators or dots in a locator (and `%` in a scope path). It cannot see symlinks, bind mounts, substituted drives, or a UNC share versus the local path behind it. It is a guard against mistakes, not a security boundary. **Requirement for T06 and A04:** resolve the real path (and check it is inside the binding's real location and scope) before calling `authorizeUse`.

## U05 notes: chat status and blocker explanations (2026-10-23)

`packages/controller/src/status/` (`StatusService`, the pure rules in `explain.ts`), a small change to `domain/blockers.ts` (a blocker's `basis`) and to `chat/` (a `status` intent). Tests: `status/status.test.ts` (18 tests) and `chat/status.test.ts` (4 tests), each on all four backends.

- **Reads only.** Every method reads records under the caller's `Principal` and writes nothing: no record, audit entry, inbox item or timer (tested by comparing the audit sequence and every task, assignment, wait, blocker, activity, evidence and pause record before and after every view), no worker is woken and no model is asked. The service holds no state, so a restart changes nothing. Each answer carries `observedAt` (the injected clock) and plain `lines`, and every line carries the records it rests on (`cites`: kind, ID, revision); a line without a cite is never produced (tested over every view).
- **Views.** `task(by, taskId)`, `overview(by, {projectId?, agentId?, state?, execution?, afterId?, limit?})`, `changes(by, {projectId, since, limit?})`, `tools(by, {taskId | projectId, since?, limit?})`, `delivered(by, {projectId, taskId?, since?})`, `noticeboard(by, projectId)`, `failedEvents(by, query)` and `inspectFailedEvent(by, handlingId)`, and `ask(by, request)` which takes one of them as an object (the chat uses it). The request objects have no actor field and unknown fields are refused.
- **Owner versus observed.** The owner of record comes from the assignment. `working` needs a sign of work no older than the freshness window (the silence threshold of the task's difficulty, the one V01 uses; `staleAfterMs` overrides it), by the owner (or the host that has been reporting stall evidence for it), under the current generation: the newer of the host activity record (H01) and the stall evidence (V01). A host that reported the work and is stale, silent or revoked makes it `unknown`. Everything else is `unknown` with the reason ("recorded as running, but no sign of work has been seen", "the last sign is 21 min old", "seen by someone who is not the owner of record", "under an earlier owner"), `assigned_not_started`, `not_assigned`, `closed` or `paused` (the gate's observed state is shown beside the intent, time promotes nothing). Time alone never makes something `working`.
- **Pending, exactly.** A task's unfinished dependencies (title, state; a cancelled prerequisite says it will never finish; the responsible agent), its open typed wait (reason, resolver, next action, deadline and what happens at it, or the indefinite owner), the retry of the announcement of its latest change when a delivery has been tried and not yet delivered (attempts, next attempt, last error), and failed event handlings of the task (next retry). A blocked task nothing explains is said to be unexplained.
- **No fabricated completion.** `completion` is `done` only when the task record says done, and `done_with_open_doubts` when an `effect_uncertain` blocker or a tool call with an unknown outcome is open for it, or when the check could not finish within its scan budget. A succeeded attempt that did not make the task done is stated as such. `delivered` lists artifacts (verified against their files through an injected verifier, a damaged one is "NOT counted as delivered"; without a verifier they are `not_checked`) and separately the tasks marked done with no artifact: done and delivered are never inferred from each other. A tool call is described as activity, not progress.
- **Blocker kind and route.** `BlockerService.openBlocker` takes an optional `basis` (`technical`, `enforced_permission`, `policy_conflict`, `owner_hold`, `preference`; stored only when stated, default `owner_hold` for `manual_hold` and `technical` for the rest). A status view shows each open blocker with its basis in words, its summary, and the route out (what has to happen and who resolves it; a policy conflict says an explicit policy change by the owner is needed and no team decision replaces it). A `preference` on a task whose outcome the owner decided (the task was created by the owner, or its latest assignment was made by the owner) is listed as `advice`, never as a blocker; on any other task it is shown with the words "a preference, not a boundary". Blockers are found by their subject: the task, an effect or attempt (their task), a task-event delivery, an inbox message (its payload), a wait ring (the tasks named in its summary; a reader who is not the owner sees a generic sentence, since the summary names every task of the ring).
- **Who sees what** (a task the reader may not read reads like a missing one; a part of a readable task the reader may not see is left out and named in `withheld`): the task, dependencies, wait, blockers and changes of tasks need `task.read` on the task (a grant narrowed to another resource does not count, an expired one neither); ownership, activity, pause and concerns need `assignment.read` or `assignment.manage` or being the assigned agent; tools used follow the MCP activity rule (owner, `history.read`, or `attempt.work` on the task); artifacts need `artifact.read`; stand-up reports need the desk rules of C01 (`desk.read`; drafts and private scratch only to their writer); the failed event queue is the event service's own rule (the owner, and those who may read the project's histories). Only the owner may leave the project out of an overview; a project-level view of a project the caller cannot read is an empty answer, the same as for one that does not exist.
- **Overview.** Filters by project, agent (with or without the `agent:` prefix; a reader who cannot see ownership never matches an agent filter), task state and execution state. Each row has the observation time and age and whether it is fresh, stale or unknown, `stale` (an attempt recorded as running with no sign of work counts), the uncertainties, the numbers of blockers and advice, the wait and the pause. Pages are cursors; a scan that ends before the page is full says `partial` with a cursor, and a blocker search that was cut says so in the answer. No scan is cut silently (tasks, assignments, waits, blockers, artifacts, desk records, effects and calls all have the same budget and marker).
- **Noticeboard.** Current assignments (ready, in progress, blocked), the latest stand-up report of each agent, and the latest deliverables, each with a link (`desk-record`, `desk`, `artifact`, `task`); the detail stays on the owner's desk. There is no stand-up record kind yet: a stand-up report is the newest shared, accepted `progress` desk record of an agent (decision, below).
- **Chat.** The interpreter may return a `status` intent (`request` as `StatusService.ask` takes it, with the owner's evidence like any other intent). `ChatService` runs it as a read under the owner's own credential (the project in focus fills in a missing project for `changes`, `delivered` and `noticeboard`; none: "Say which project you mean."), stores the outcome (`answered`, at most 30 lines, `moreLines` says how many were left out) and renders the reply with the citations. A replay of the same message returns the first answer, not a fresh one. Quoted text cannot ask (the usual `untrusted_authority`). Without `ChatDeps.status` the answer is `unavailable`. A status answer needs no coordinator and no model.
- **Decisions I made.** (1) A blocker's `basis` is a new optional field rather than derived: nothing recorded says whether a block is a permission or a policy conflict, and a preference cannot be told from a technical block by its text. Existing blockers keep working (technical, or the person's hold). (2) "Owner-decided outcome" means the LATEST assignment was made by the owner or, with no assignment, the task was created by the owner; a later reassignment by a supervisor makes it not owner-decided. (3) A stand-up report is a shared, accepted `progress` desk record. (4) Tool and effect lists, and blocker details and doubts about outside actions, follow the MCP activity rule rather than `task.read`: a reader denied tools sees a blocker on an effect, attempt, delivery or message with a generic summary (the effect, destination and error text are not shown), is told in `withheld`, and sees a done task as `done_with_open_doubts` with the generic line "an action's outcome is not known" when a tool call has no known outcome. (5) A dependency on an unfinished prerequisite the reader may not read is shown as "waiting on something you cannot see" (no title, no ID). (4b) A stated blocker `basis` is accepted as given for now; deriving it from the opener's role is a later improvement. (6) `ask` returns undefined, and the chat says "nothing you may read", for a missing or unreadable subject. (7) Not built, as instructed: the Notice, Needs you and Urgent levels (U11b).
- **Limits.** The freshness window is a provisional per-difficulty default (V01's). Changes are derived from the current state of the records and their update times, not from a log, so "what changed" says what a record is now and when it last changed; a record changed twice shows once. Records that cannot be told apart by time (the task record has one `updatedAt`) show their latest state only. Scans read whole kinds in ID order (tasks, assignments, waits, blockers, artifacts, desk records, effects), bounded by `scanPages` x `scanPageSize` (25 x 200 by default) per kind, so on a very large installation `partial` is the honest answer and an index record would be needed (a later card). The effect list scans all effects for the project's tasks. The coordinator's model route list is not used by status. Event text shown by `inspectFailedEvent` is outside data, redacted and bounded at 300 characters per event, and the line says it is not an instruction. A chat status outcome stores its lines in the turn (bounded at 30 lines); the replay therefore shows the old observation time, which the reply states.
- **After review.** Nothing opens a `preference` blocker yet: the advice rule is exercised only by tests, and the first card that records a supervisor or team preference as a blocker must pass `basis: "preference"`. A `manual_hold` blocker can be opened by any holder of `blocker.manage`, so "a hold placed by a person" is only true when the opener is the owner; the status words name an agent, host or service opener instead (a latent issue: the route text "only a person lifts it" still comes from the type). Reader gaps are named: the task view lists ownership, pause and tools in `withheld`, and the overview has its own `withheld` list. Stand-up reports older than 36 hours (`standupStaleAfterMs`) are flagged as old. Answers avoid generation numbers, round numbers and raw stream IDs ("since it was last reassigned", "recent" / "old" / "not heard from lately"). The interpreter's status request is bounded (at most 8 short alphabetic keys, values only numbers, booleans or text up to 200 characters, no nesting).

## M01 notes: durable meeting scheduling and agendas (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/meetings/` (`MeetingService`, the pure calendar arithmetic in `zones.ts`), two test files (`meetings.test.ts` 28 tests on each of the four backends, `zones.test.ts` 11 pure tests). Mutation-checked (list in the report). Scope is scheduling and agendas only: the meeting procedure (chair, motions, votes, transcript, summaries; M05/M06) is not built and waits for the owner's acceptance of the M00 record.

- **Schedules.** A `meeting-schedule` is a wall-clock time in a named IANA zone with a recurrence (`once`, `daily`, `weekly` on chosen days), optional start and end dates, a length, a preparation lead (the occurrence is created that long before the start so agents can reach a safe boundary and prepare), a fixed roster of agent principals and a planning budget figure (stored, not enforced). The zone is the schedule's own or, when it names none, the owner's `meeting.timeZone` setting (`MEETING_SETTINGS` are extra definitions for `ConfigurationService`; `ownerTimeZoneFrom` reads it); the zone is copied into the schedule when it is made, so changing the setting later changes no schedule. Zone names are kept as written (the runtime's preferred spelling of an alias differs between platforms).
- **DST-safe.** All arithmetic is `Intl` with the injected clock, no dependency. A schedule is "09:00 in Berlin", never "every 24 hours". A local time that does not exist (spring-forward gap) happens just after the gap; a repeated one (fall-back) happens at its first pass; a local date has at most one slot, so a repeated hour cannot make two meetings. Tested with fixed zones: Europe/Berlin and America/New_York across both changes, Australia/Lord_Howe (half-hour change), Pacific/Auckland, Asia/Kolkata and UTC.
- **Idempotent occurrences, restart and catch-up.** `tick` (owner, host or service principal only, never a task credential) looks at each active schedule: the slots after the schedule's cursor whose preparation window has opened are *due*; the latest becomes one `meeting-occurrence`, named by schedule and local date, created in the same commit that moves the cursor past it. All earlier due slots are skipped in favour of it and recorded (`skippedCount`, the last ten dates, `skippedTruncated` when the 400-day look-back was hit; `coversFrom` says what period a report covers). The command ID carries the schedule revision, so a repeat at the same state replays and a tick after an edit decides again. Several processes ticking at once, repeated ticks and a restarted service make exactly one occurrence per slot (tested with concurrent ticks). An occurrence whose end has already passed is `mode: catch-up`, created already settled as `state: reported`: a report for the period, no meeting to call people into, no agenda taken, no invitations, and no place in the open lists (so any number of outages cannot fill the caps or block `callUrgent`; `planned()` does not list it).
- **Clock loop, not built.** Nothing calls `tick` by itself; the controller's own loop must (owner/host/service principal). `tick` returns `nextDueAt` (the earliest next creation among the schedules it looked at, only claimed for a complete pass), `next` (continue from this schedule when the limit of 50, at most 200, was reached) and `blocked` (schedules that could not make their occurrence because their project has 200 waiting meetings or the installation 1000). `tick` also returns `skipped`: slots not made because an edit or resume overtook them (an edit marks `editedAt`; slots at or before it are not made) or because the date already had its meeting (lead decision: an edit that moves the time on a date that already has an occurrence makes no second meeting, and the tick says so). A planned live meeting that is neither held nor cancelled 60 minutes after its end is marked `missed` on the next first-page tick, and its agenda items go back to the queue (the lease for anything that could keep items captive).
- **Urgent meetings.** `callUrgent` (needs `meeting.manage`) creates a live occurrence at the clock's now for an explicit roster (resolving "the team" to agents is the caller's, as in the chat); the topic becomes an urgent agenda item attached to it, as do the queued urgent items whose intended participants include someone on the roster. Idempotent by command ID.
- **Absent and paused participants, no unauthorized wake.** The roster is fixed when the occurrence is made (known, active agents, each once; no later edit adds a voice). Each participant is `eligible`, `withheld-paused` (the required `availability` hook, in production `PauseService.availability`, which fails closed: a missing or throwing hook withholds everyone as unavailable and `inviteDirect` is refused; the agent's own gate, its team chain's or the installation's), `withheld-unavailable` (suspended or stopped) or `owner-invited`. Nothing in this service wakes, resumes or starts anything; invitations go out only through `dispatchInvitations` (owner/host/service), which decides availability again first (paused since creation: withheld; resumed since: now eligible, so a resumed agent can join late) and hands each invitation, once, to an injected `InvitationSink` with a stable key (`<occurrence>:<agent>`). Delivery is at least once: the participant is recorded as invited only after the sink returned, a sink that dies is retried with the same keys. The sink is the thing that would message the agent or start a bounded meeting task; it is not built here. Only the owner can invite one paused agent by hand (`inviteDirect`): bounded attendance for that meeting, and the pause stays (tested: availability is still `paused`). A suspended or stopped agent is never woken by an invitation (refused); that is the owner's wake or recovery decision. The agent's saved report is not generated here (M05/M06).
- **Agenda.** `agenda-item` records follow the README: topic, the requester's own question, context, requested outcome, urgency (`normal`/`urgent`), intent (`discussion`, `recommendation`, `delegated-decision`, `owner-instruction`), intended participants, owner, a link to where it came from (an inquiry the requester can see (H04), validated through `InquiryService.view`, or a chat turn (U03), which only the owner or a project meeting manager may link and which shows nothing of its content), state `queued`, `scheduled` (attached to an occurrence), `discussed`, `deferred` (reason and next step required) or `resolved` (final, optional outcome link). A topic recorded as an owner instruction or a decision delegated to the team needs the owner: a supervisor's paraphrase cannot turn a tentative idea into an instruction. Open items (queued or deferred) are kept in one index record per project (200 at most, then `unavailable`), so no scan is truncated; `openAgenda` orders the whole list before paging. Items returned by a settlement that no longer fit stay queued/deferred marked `unlisted` and are counted in the meeting's `agendaUnlisted` (`setAgendaState` to `queued` lists one again when there is room). An occurrence takes at most 25 matching open items, urgent first, then oldest; the rest stay open and are counted (`agendaOverflow`). **Agenda retained:** cancelling or missing a meeting returns its items to the queue; settling it as held defers the ones it did not reach with a reason and next step; catch-up reports take none; deferred items come back on the next agenda.
- **Authorization.** New actions `meeting.manage` (schedules, urgent meetings, settling by cancel, changing items), `meeting.agenda` (adding items, changing one's own) and `meeting.read`; the owner may do all of it. Per project: a grant for A says nothing about B. Reads of what the viewer may not see look like missing records (schedules, items, occurrences; an occurrence is also visible to its own roster). `settle ... held`, `tick`, `dispatchInvitations` are for owner/host/service only. No operation takes an actor, resolver or principal from the caller. Text is bounded and credential-shaped text is refused.
- **Open-meetings bookkeeping.** Waiting live meetings are listed per project (`meeting-open/<project>`, 200 at most) with a global record (`meeting-open/all`: count 1000 at most, and the projects that have any, which the sweep walks). Every creation and settlement writes both under revision checks, so creations in different projects contend on the global record and the loser retries; that is the cost of the global cap. Far-ahead schedules (a once date or start date beyond 400 days) stay active; the first slot is found from that date.
- **Lead decisions recorded.** Only the owner may invite a paused agent for now (a manager route is a later settings item); marking a meeting held stays owner/host/service until M05 gives the chair that right; caps 200 per project, 1000 global, 60-minute grace, 400-day look-back accepted. A chat-turn link must belong to the project; a missing and a foreign turn get the same refusal.
- **Limits stated.** Meeting *running* is not built: no chair, no transcript, no brief, no summary, no vote; `held` is set by whoever runs the meeting (owner/host/service). Recording what a meeting decided is the caller's `setAgendaState` with an `outcomeRef` string; the decision record itself is M06. The invitation sink, the clock loop and a chat command for "meeting now" are wiring for later cards (no chat command was added, since `CHAT_OPS` maps only to existing state-API operations). Urgent meetings use UTC for their `localDate` and `timeZone` fields. A task-level pause is not consulted (a task pause does not stop its agent from attending). The 60-minute grace, 25 items per meeting, 50 members per roster and 400-day look-back are constants in `MEETING_LIMITS`. A schedule edit that moves the time marks `editedAt`: a meeting that would have fallen at or before the edit is not made, and the next tick reports it as skipped. No UI, no notification to the owner when a meeting is missed (the `missed` state is the record).
- **What later cards must know.** M05/M06 should create nothing for an occurrence itself: take the `planned` occurrence (`planned()` lists a project's), run it, and call `settle(..., "held")` (agenda items left `scheduled` are deferred automatically) and `setAgendaState` for each item. The occurrence's `participants` are the roster of record for quorum: `withheld-*` participants are absent, not abstaining; an `owner-invited` one attends bounded.


## G04 notes: durable engine integration proof (2026-10-22)

`packages/integration/src/g04-*` and `docs/nimblenewt/implementation-plan/g04-evidence.md` (what is and is not proven, results, mutation checks). A proof card: no production behaviour changed.

- **What it is.** Tests over the production S09 slice, S08 `WaitService` and the real Pi adapter on SQLite, SQLite on a worker thread and PostgreSQL: real SIGKILL of controller plus Pi at every boundary (`g04-kill-*`), the Pi adapter in a separate worker process killed in flight and after the answer, controller crash at every boundary with the worker alive (`g04-process-*`), injected rollbacks and ambiguous commits at each of 41 operations (`g04-faults-*`), 218 real connection cuts on PostgreSQL (`g04-connection-postgres`), and waits across restarts (`g04-wait-*`). Every run must end in the uncrashed run's state, with every acknowledged operation present in the raw database before any command is re-run.
- **Files.** `g04-fixture.ts` (store specs, engines, `WorkerHost`, the acknowledged-operation check), `g04-wait.ts` (wait scenario), `g04-worker.ts` / `g04-child.ts` (child processes); the `*-suite.ts` files hold the tests that the per-database `*.test.ts` files instantiate (one file per database to stay under the per-file time limit).
- **Changes outside the package.** `controller/src/slice/index.ts` additionally exports `startTestServer`, `withClient`, `FaultProxy` for the integration tests; `storage/postgres/testing/proxy.ts` sets `noDelay` on its sockets (the relay cost ~25 ms a statement; test support only).
- **Result.** No defect in production code and no evidence against the selected architecture (see the evidence file for the classification rule). Do not read it as more than it is: scripted model, process death not power loss, PostgreSQL server never killed, the protected effect runs through the slice's target, not a Pi tool.
- **For later cards.** A reconciler pass that meets a storage error counts it and ends without throwing, so callers and tests must judge completion by state. The recovery of a lost connection during `startProcess` (epoch acquisition) needs a new start. Operation IDs of effect claims contain the holder name, so a restarted process names its claims differently (tests name the clean run's holder like the killed process's). The fixed boundary count (127) is derived in the tests, not hard-coded: a change to the path changes the labels, not the assertions.
- **Default and exhaustive.** By default the files run a sample (key boundaries plus every eighth/fifth, see the evidence file), up to about 35 s a file. `g04-kill-sqlite` and `g04-kill-postgres` are in `scripts/slow-tests.json`. `g04-kill-sqlite-worker` and the `g04-process-*` files are skipped unless `NIMBLENEWT_G04_HEAVY=1` or `NIMBLENEWT_G04_EXHAUSTIVE=1`. `NIMBLENEWT_G04_EXHAUSTIVE=1` runs everything (at least 14 minutes for all files); the `g04-exhaustive` job in `qa.yml` (input `job` = `g04` or `both`) does that, with the heavy files, on the hosted runners.
## C08 notes: owner decision records and correction propagation (2026-10-22)

`packages/controller/src/decisions/` (`DecisionService`, `records.ts`), `decisions.test.ts` with 29 tests on each of the four backends (116 runs). Small edits elsewhere (all covered by those tests): a new link kind `owner-decision` in the context layer's `SourceRef` (retrieval, branches and compaction labels), an optional `decisions` guard in `GenerationService` (a refusal reason `decision`, a test seam `afterDecisionRead`) and in `ReviewService` (`ReviewOptions.decisions`), and the ID prefixes `dec`, `dqn`, `dsp`. Mutation-checked, see the end.

- **What a decision is.** An `owner-decision` record: the owner's own wording (up to 2,000 characters) and the reading of it (`statement`), a category (`decision`, `goal`, `permission` the owner stated, or `gate`), a topic (folded to lower case), a scope (the project or one task), a version within its topic and scope, `supersedes`, `supersededBy`, where it came from (a chat turn, or the API directly) and who said it, from authentication (the owner's principal and credential digest; `session` is a label the client gives its session or device, shown and never trusted as an identity). They are separate records from the agents' desk records of kind `decision`, which are conclusions and never authoritative. Only the authenticated owner records one (agents, hosts, services and clients are `forbidden`; no actor, resolver or principal ID is accepted from the caller).
- **What is not a decision.** `stance` (how the interpreter read the message) must be `decided`; brainstorm, hypothetical, quoted, paraphrase and question create nothing and return `recorded: false`. As a backstop that does not trust the interpreter, wording with brainstorming or hypothetical markers ("what if", "for example", "maybe we could", "not decided" and the like) or ending in a question mark records nothing either. For a chat source the controller reads the turn: it must be the owner's own turn and the wording must be a phrase of the owner's own text, which never contains quoted material (`>` lines, fenced blocks, client-marked quotes). Credential-shaped text is refused. The wording check is U03's `evidenceInText`/`evidenceStrong`.
- **A correction.** A later decision on the same topic supersedes the active decisions in its scope in the same commit, so it is authoritative at once: a project-wide decision supersedes the project's earlier one and the task-level ones on that topic; a task-level one only that task's. Other topics and other tasks are untouched. Superseded decisions stay, marked `superseded` and pointing at what replaced them; `history` lists every version of a topic. The same statement said again records nothing and answers with the existing decision. Limits: 400 versions per topic, 50 decisions superseded at once, 200 authoritative decisions per project.
- **Concurrent sessions (`basedOn`).** Every decision names the version of its topic and scope the owner's session saw (0 for none). If the decision moved meanwhile and the words differ, nothing is overwritten: both become `disputed` (neither authoritative), a dispute record opens, work that relied on them is blocked, tasks that relied on the earlier one are told (an event) and a running generation is interrupted, a further decision on the topic is refused, and only the owner's explicit `resolveDispute` (choose one; the other is superseded) settles it. Never arrival order, never a vote. Two sessions at the same moment are tested: exactly one sees the other.
- **Questions.** `ask` (an agent working a task) returns the owner's decision when the topic is decided, the dispute when it is disputed, and the open question when one exists; otherwise it opens one question per topic. The owner's decision on the topic answers and closes it; the owner can `dismissQuestion`. Open questions are in the context record (at most 50 per project, 10 per topic).
- **Links, and what blocks.** `link` ties a plan (a desk record of kind plan), a task contract, a review, an action (an effect) or a branch of a task to the decisions it relies on (only authoritative decisions of the task's project that apply to the task; the worker needs `attempt.work` on the task, or is the owner). Whether work may go on is read from the decisions themselves at that moment (`guard`), not from a flag set later: from the commit of a correction, an effect cannot be admitted, started or approved for a task whose work relies on a decision that is no longer authoritative, in a busy, paused or restarted agent alike, and it stays so until `revalidate` links the work to the current decision. Starting a generation (thinking) is not blocked by stale links, so the agent can read the new decision and revalidate. `revalidate` needs, for every stale link, the current authoritative decision on its topic; a superseded or disputed decision cannot be named, so no agent agreement, team vote or repetition restores old guidance. A stale link to a review can be revalidated only by someone other than the review's requester (a reviewer or the owner). The guard returns revision checks that commit with the caller's change, so a correction racing an admission lands strictly before or after it (tested with a seam).
- **Gates.** A `gate` decision (topic `implementation-gate`; `planning-only`, `implementation-hold` or `open`) binds by itself, with no link needed, at project or task level (the task's own gate wins). `implementation-hold` refuses a new generation and every effect; `planning-only` refuses effects (planning itself is allowed). A disputed gate refuses both. Only a later owner decision lifts it. Wired into `GenerationService.begin/admitEffect/execute/guardFor` and into `ReviewService.decide` (an allow) and `admit`.
- **Propagation (`propagate`, `resume`).** After a correction commits, a resumable pass (a `decision-propagation` record listed in the project's context record until done) does for each task that relied on a replaced decision: marks its links `superseded` (naming the new decision); records each finished, uncertain or already started action as a follow-up (`undone: false` always; closed as `addressed` with an optional corrective effect by the owner or the working agent, or `accepted` by the owner only; nothing ever marks an effect undone); interrupts a running generation with L05's `correct` (so a late response cannot apply and an admitted, unstarted effect is withdrawn); and publishes `decision-superseded`, `decision-disputed` or `decision-resolved` on the task's event stream (H06), which a host reads when it next wakes the agent, also after a pause or a restart. Each step has a command ID derived from the propagation and the task, so a repeat does nothing twice; progress is saved per task; a step that fails (stream backpressure, a missing right) leaves the propagation pending and `resume` (owner, host or service; call it at start-up) finishes it. Repeating the correcting command also finishes it. `propagate` and `resume` are gated to owner, host and service; interrupting a generation needs `assignment.manage` on the task, so a service principal without it leaves that step pending.
- **Context record.** `context(project, task?)` is the versioned record of current decisions, goals, permissions the owner stated, gates, unresolved conflicts and open questions, read from the records, never from a summary, so it is the same after any number of compactions. Its version rises on every change. `startupItems` turns it into the C07 builder's items: the owner's words and gates and conflicts are `human` (always whole), `permission` and `goal` keep their kinds, questions are cuttable; superseded decisions are not included. A task sees the project-wide decisions and its own.
- **Links to the original wording.** `SourceRef` has a third kind `{ kind: "owner-decision", id }`: resolved with the reader's rights (the owner; history.read in the project; agents working the decision's task), as text that begins `[AUTHORITATIVE]` or `[NOT AUTHORITATIVE, superseded by ...]` followed by the owner's own words, so C07b's `pinned` decisions, branch updates (C05) and delegations carry it. Its digest includes the state, so a branch that took a decision in sees it as `changed` after a correction. Tested through three compactions and a correction.
- **U10.** Nothing in configuration touches these records; applying a layer leaves decisions, disputes and questions as they were (tested).
- **Decisions I made (for the owner to confirm).** (1) A project-wide correction also supersedes task-level decisions on the same topic (the task-level one is not kept as an exception). (2) `planning-only` allows planning (a generation may begin) but no effect; the card's "admits no work" is read as no implementation work. (3) A disputed topic blocks only work that is linked to it, and gates; unlinked work is not blocked, because the controller cannot know what an action is about. (4) A task's agent may revalidate its own plans, actions and branches (it can only name the current decision); a review needs someone else. (5) Choosing the decision the work already relied on needs no revalidation.
